#156. 'An annoying buzz': The search for stealthier drones

Amazon Prime Air is expanding to 500 US towns and cities

Nature lover Ashley Schmidt no longer spends much time in her garden, in the Dallas suburb of North Garland.

She's also given up on cycling to the local parks where she previously enjoyed taking close-up photos of the bugs, lizards and toads.

It's all because she can't stand the sound of the delivery drones that fly overhead, depositing her neighbours' online orders.

"They emit this very annoying buzz. It triggers my fight or flight response," says the 44-year-old administrative assistant.

"You can hear the drones on approach and you can hear them inside your home when they pass directly overhead and then you hear them as they leave," she says.

Amazon says it is working to reduce the noise of its drone fleet

Schmidt is one of a growing number of people living with the noise of drones dispatched by companies including Amazon and Walmart as they seek to speed up home deliveries.

Delivery drone services, not long ago a novelty, are becoming part of everyday life for many people in the US.

Among them, Amazon Prime Air is in the process of expanding deliveries to nearly 500 cities and towns, delivering items that fit into a space the size of a shoe box. It has also launched to customers in and around Darlington in the north of England.

Smaller drone delivery services are popping up too: in some US cities, customers can order flying treats from their local pizza joint or ice cream parlour.

Even in cities where noise is already a steady part of life, the sound of drones seems to irk in a particular way.

While the steady passing of traffic might blend into the background, the sudden whine of a drone's rotors can pierce the everyday hum.

The sound tends to be "particularly noticeable, even when overall sound levels are relatively low," says Antonio Torija Martinez, professor in acoustic engineering and psychoacoustics at the University of Salford.

Unlike planes, drones operate at low altitudes and may fly directly over residential areas.

"They can expose communities that have not previously experienced significant aircraft noise, and who may therefore be more sensitive to changes in their local soundscape," says Torija Martinez.

He points to research showing that people respond strongly to a noise when they consider the source to be "novel, intrusive or frequent". People who benefit from a drone delivery service, for example, may be less bothered by the noise.

However, complaints about the sound of drones are likely to grow, he warns: "At the moment we are seeing some of these problems localised in some areas, butâ¦ as the drone sector expands, more and more of these complaints will happen."

In the US, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) regulates commercial drones, but it has come under fire for allowing noisy drone delivery operations. The FAA says operators and local community leaders have primary responsibility for addressing any community concerns.

Greenjets encloses some of the blades on its drone inside a pod

Might better technology muffle the irritating buzz?

One startup, Greenjets, set out to tackle both "noise level and the frequency of that noise," as Darren James, future technologies lead at the company, puts it.

The London-based business has designed a propulsion system it claims can reduce drone noise by at least two thirds.

While standard drones use spinning propellers that slice through open air, creating that high-pitched buzz, Greenjets encloses spinning blades inside a pod, to muffle the sound and direct the noise away from the ground.

Its system also quietens things down through slower rotor speed and by using materials that reduce vibration noise.

Making quieter drones isn't just about keeping the noise down for neighbours who are already sick of airborne deliveries.

Greenjets' low-noise tech has been used by police and harbour security teams in Germany. It has also helped conservationists in Africa, who are seeking to track poachers from above without being detected.

Battlefield drone operators also have good reason to keep the noise down, to avoid alerting the enemy of the approach of their unmanned vehicles.

"There are some efforts to reduce the sound of drones. The US Special Forces are interested, at an experimental stage," says Robert Tollast, a research fellow at Whitehall think tank The Royal United Services Institute (RUSI).

But he stresses that drone speed, rather than stealthiness, tends to come first for larger drones used in the war between Russia and Ukraine. "Make it faster, not less quiet, seems to be the approach," says Tollast.

Sharrow Engineering developed twisted propellers that reduce noise

For Detroit-based entrepreneur, Greg Sharrow, it was the challenge of broadcasting a classical music performance back in 2012 that set him on the path to quieter drone design.

He needed to film a violinist from a tricky angle and a drone seemed the only solution â except that it would be far too noisy.

As a starting point, "I knew that most of the noise from the drone was coming from the propeller, not the motor," he says. Specifically, it was the tips of the blades that he realised were creating the noise as they swiped through the air.

"I asked myself a very simple question, what would a tipless propeller look like and how would it function?"

The result was to create a propeller with twisted loop-shaped blades with no points.

It turned out it was easier to test his propeller in water than it was to repeatedly fly a drone, which led Sharrow to initially develop a less noisy boat propeller. Now, however, his company, Sharrow Engineering, is focusing on developing "ultra-quiet" drone rotors.

"We have validated that the technology works in drone applications and that it is dramatically quieter," says Sharrow.

Companies at the forefront of drone deliveries say they are also working to reduce the noise of their fleets. A spokesperson for Amazon says it has made "measurable sound improvements" to its drones and its latest designs use custom-designed propellers "that produce almost half the perceived sound of the prior generation, with a milder tone".

Drone maker Wing, which provides its vehicles for Walmart deliveries, says its propellers are designed to minimise noise. It also varies its flight paths to prevent repeated overflights over any single home or neighbourhood.

For Schmidt, fed up with the noise of drones, the only truly satisfactory solution would be to halt drone deliveries in her area. "I would like them to completely stop until they figure out how their services stop being a nuisance," she says.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#155. 'I live with a ghost' - inside the haunted homes attracting bargain hunters

The previous occupant of Ryua's apartment, pictured here, sadly took their own life

From the moment Ryua first walked into her rental apartment in Tokyo she believed it to be haunted.

"When I visited the place for a viewing there was a man standing there, glaring at me," she says.

"Since the estate agent couldn't see him, I realised he must be a ghost."

While someone more squeamish would probably have quickly left the flat never to return, Ryua chose to go ahead and move in.

"I'm a bit of a contrarian, so I decided I wanted to become friends with this glaring male ghost," adds Ryua, who works as an aidoru, a young entertainer, and who doesn't wish to share her surname.

Four years later, and still in the same apartment, she says she continues to experience paranormal activity on a daily basis. She says she hears endless footsteps and the ghost talking.

Ryua says that she shares her Tokyo apartment with a ghost

Although Japan has increasingly marked Halloween, external in recent years, many Japanese people take ghosts seriously all year round. The country has a word, "yuurei", which means a lingering, troubled spirit.

These are the ghosts of people who died in violent or tragic circumstances - those who were murdered, killed themselves, or simply were lonely and died alone. If they died at home, then the belief is that their yuurei won't go away.

For house hunters who are not superstitious or squeamish it means there are big bargains to be found. There is a whole category of dwellings called jiko bukken, which means "stigmatised" or "incident" properties.

Under guidelines issued by the government in 2021, Japanese estate agents must disclose suicide, murder, death by fire, and any death where the body was not discovered for an extended period of time.

For rental properties this information must be disclosed for three years, and it must be revealed indefinitely for any property offered for sale.

By contrast, in the UK there is no legal requirement, external to reveal that a person died in a property. And in the US, it differs from state to state., external For example, in California you have to reveal if a person has died in the property in the past three years, while in Arizona and Oregon you do not.

Ryua was told that a man had killed himself in her apartment. It meant that she gets an almost 30% discount on her rent on her tiny, 25 sq m bedsit in the fashionable ward of Setagaya. She says she pays 45,000 yen ($285; Â£213) a month, instead of what should have been 62,000 yen.

While a house where there had been a murder or suicide would probably be harder to sell or rent out anywhere in the world, Tokyo estate agent Erik Oskamp says that Japanese people are particularly superstitious and averse to stigmatised properties.

"[Even] an apartment next to a cemetery will be difficult to rent out, especially if you can see the tombs from the kitchen," explains Oskamp. "The worst thing is if a suicide has happened in the apartment. Even if somebody has jumped from the roof, many renters will move out. Other issues are a serious crime or a lonely death."

The latter issue, that of a person dying alone, would not generally affect the price of a property in, say, the UK or the US.

But in rural Kagoshima, in Japan's southwest, it enabled Diane Tincher to buy a large three-bedroom house for about half of what would have otherwise been the asking price.

The 69-year-old American has lived in Japan for nearly 40 years, working as a tour guide. She says that the estate agent had been straight with her â there was a heavy discount as an elderly lady had died alone, thereby stigmatising the property.

Tincher paid 20m yen for the house, in a location where similar properties usually sell for up to 45m yen.

"I don't believe in ghosts. But I know that the Japanese are very superstitious," she says. "If there is a ghost I'm thankful to her every day... to live in such a splendid and affordable home."

Diane Tincher says she got a 50% reduction on her home in rural Japan

Researching the house, Tincher had found it on a website called Oshimaland, which lists jiko bukken properties, giving details of the events that occurred. It was launched in 2005 covering Tokyo, before expanding across Japan and then internationally in 2011.

It is crowdsourced, meaning that members of the public add the homes and their details.

Diane Tincher got a huge discount on her home in rural Japan

Teru Oshima, the founder of Oshimaland, says that in the past such properties were "not very attractive options" in Japan.

Yet as house prices have surged in the country in recent years, as well as a big increase in the wider cost of living, one 2025 survey found that more than half of Japanese people would now rent a jiko bukken if it was cheap., external

Back in Setagaya, Ryua says she is happy living with her ghostly flatmate. "I believe that ghosts were once living human beings, so they deserve our respect," she says.

#153. U.S. midterm elections in pictures: Democrats' sweeping victories deliver rebuke to Trump

Democrats scored sweeping victories in the U.S. midterm elections on Wednesday, flipping key Republican-held seats in Congress and securing historic wins in traditionally conservative states, as voters delivered a setback to President Donald Trump.

The party was projected to gain control of both chambers of Congress, with victories across several battleground states threatening to constrain Trump's legislative agenda for the remaining two years of his presidency.

Here are some of the scenes from polling stations, campaign headquarters and election-night celebrations across the United States.

Among the biggest surprises was Texas, where Democrat James Talarico defeated Republican Ken Paxton to become the state's first Democratic U.S. senator since 1988.

Fellow Democrat Gina Hinojosa also unseated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, becoming the state's first Latina governor and ending more than three decades of Republican control of the office.

In North Carolina, former Democratic Gov. Roy Cooper flipped a Republican-held Senate seat after defeating Trump-backed Michael Whatley. Cooper will succeed retiring Republican Sen. Thom Tillis, who announced his departure after clashing with Trump over his tax and spending legislation.

In Georgia, Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff secured reelection against Trump-backed Republican Mike Collins, while former Atlanta Mayor Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governor's race, becoming the first Black woman elected governor of a U.S. state.

Meanwhile, in Ohio, former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown staged a political comeback, defeating Republican incumbent Jon Husted in a closely watched Senate race.

The elections were widely viewed as a referendum on Trump's second term, with concerns over the cost of living, inflation and the war in Iran weighing on voter sentiment.

—CNBC's Danielle DeVries contributed to this article.

#152. How video game remakes tap the power of nostalgia

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is often rated as one of the greatest video games

The moment she saw footage from the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, Kacey Doran picked up her phone. She had to tell her sister.

"Have you seen this?" messaged Doran, excitedly.

Nearly 30 years ago, a 10-year-old Doran had sat in front of her family's TV, reading out the dialogue on screen to her sister, a year younger, as they inched their way through the original Nintendo 64 game's sprawling, puzzle-filled dungeons. Ocarina's fantasy-like story, and beautiful music, enthralled them both.

It was also the first game Doran ever completed. Now, countless memories rushed back to her as she devoured clips of the fully redesigned version, due to arrive on the Switch 2 later this year.

Doran's sister messaged back: "I'm not tearing upâ¦ YOU'RE tearing up."

News of a video game remake bought back happy memories for Kacey Doran

This is the power of nostalgia â and a perfect example of how video games can trigger it. Doran now teaches an esports course at Rowan University in New Jersey. She says the original Ocarina of Time, released in 1998, had a "magical" quality that she hopes to rediscover, and also share with her sister's children.

The video games industry has long leaned on remakes. From Mario Kart to the Final Fantasy series, or even Pac-Man, some games just keep coming back in updated forms, as new generations of gamers emerge and as computer hardware improves, enabling more detailed graphics and increasingly expansive game worlds.

While the emotional hook may be the most significant draw, putting out a remake can be a double-edged sword. Some gamers are very protective of their favourite titles and new versions don't always meet certain fans' astronomically high expectations.

Nostalgia is an important but mixed emotion, says Tim Wulf, at the Centre for Advanced Internet Studies in Bochum, Germany. It has positive aspects but also a kind of sadness, too. Happiness in remembering things that were important to us, and also an awareness that that time is now gone.

"It's a social emotion because memories that induce nostalgia for us are connected to other people," he explains, adding that nostalgia can boost our self-esteem while highlighting key social bonds.

Video games, says Wulf, are notoriously good at triggering nostalgia because gamers can "jump into the same environment". In other words, games are like time machines for our emotions, and returning to fondly remembered places can feel stabilising or reassuring in an ever-changing world.

Doran, for one, notes that when you return to a favourite old game, there's something satisfying about deploying previously learned skills. You know what to do here, you're winning again. Just like the old days.

Video games are good at triggering nostalgic feelings says Tim Wulf

Putting out remasters and remakes has been a "really successful" strategy for the games industry, says Piers Harding-Rolls, head of games research at Ampere Analysis.

"Tying in with established audiences and building on existing content is quite a safe route for a lot of companies," he adds. In many cases, it is cheaper to update an existing game rather than create a new title from scratch.

Among other remakes that have found big audiences recently are those of Resident Evil 4 and Assassin's Creed Black Flag.

But Ocarina of Time perhaps stands out because, ever since its original release, it has been considered by many as one of the greatest games ever made. "The interest from all the Gen Zs that I work with is really high," says Harding-Rolls. The legend of The Legend of Zelda, as it were.

Since 2007 there have been 14 main releases of Assassin's Creed

Clearly, nostalgia is not the only driver of interest in the remake, and today's audiences will expect a game that plays to today's standards.

Games companies must keep innovating when linking contemporary audiences to the past, says Wulf, in order satisfy their desire for fresh experiences. And although Wulf himself was a big fan of the original, he has no personal interest in the Ocarina of Time remake. "I want a new game â I don't want to play an old game," he says. Nostalgia isn't always enough for some.

Plus, critics often highlight a tension between originals and remakes. Which is the definitive version? In 2021, Square Enix released the bizarrely titled Nier Replicant ver.1.22474487139... â a remaster of a game first published in 2010. One reviewer wrote, external, "What we're left with is two nearly identical Niers each vying to be the true Nier."

Fans may react badly should a remaster or remake fail to do justice to the source material. And, in the gaming world, angry fan reactions can be serious.

"There's always going to be that fan base or group of people that are going to nit-pick," says Stephanie Ijoma, chief executive and founder of NNESAGA, a consultancy and creative agency.

Ultimately, games developers working on remakes have to "find the right balance" between appealing to fans and allowing themselves to be creative with the material, she adds. "You can't make art in fear, you can't create in fear."

Ijoma gives the example of Final Fantasy VII. First released in 1997, it got a highly anticipated remake in 2020.

This was a game Ijoma played back in the late 1990s and she was thrilled to be able to share it with younger players today.

First released in 1997, Final Fantasy VII got a remake in 2020

Remakes enable this sharing of experiences because getting old computer hardware or video games consoles to work, while a pastime loved by some, is not possible for everyone.

"I've tried multiple times to play Silent Hill 2 because I'm interested in older games but it's such a difficult game to come into nowadays," says Niklas Hallin, a solo indie game developer who runs the studio Neckbolt. "They made a remake and it's so smooth, it just works. It works on your modern hardware."

Hallin did not play many games as a child so he says nostalgia isn't much of a motivator for him. However, in his own games, he likes to include subtle stylistic references to classic games because he knows that this appeals to gamers.

Indie developers often lean on retro games aesthetics not just because people respond well to them â but also because they are easier to achieve. "You can't compete with billion dollar budgets, super high-fidelity, Triple A graphics," he says.

One question remains. The visual difference, today, between games released years apart is, arguably, negligible. So will there be any point in remaking a game from 2026 in, say, 2038? Will technology have moved on so much by then that it's actually worth it?

Harding-Rolls predicts that games companies will still seek to connect new audiences with classic titles by offering updates.

Remakes might become less about graphical improvements and more about editorial adjustments. "Reinterpretations," he says. "Or versions with additional content."

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

The start-ups hoping to return battery making to the US

#151. Versant earnings beat expectations as digital platforms drive growth

Versant Media on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that beat expectations and reaffirmed its full-year 2026 guidance, citing strength across its portfolio and growth in its platforms revenue.

The media company reported $245 million in revenue from digital platforms including Fandango, marking a nearly 10% year-over-year increase. Versant said Fandango saw "significant growth" in ticketing revenue in the third quarter, notching its highest level since the pandemic.

Advertising revenue, however, slipped to $373 million during the period, down 5.3% from last year, primarily due to the impact of last year's Ryder Cup, according to the company.

The Versant portfolio includes CNBC, MS NOW, Golf Channel and other cable and digital properties.

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter, compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Versant completed four major distribution renewals this year following its spinoff from Comcast's NBCUniversal.

During the third quarter, Versant said, its networks reached more than 120 million viewers monthly and its aggregate viewership grew by double digits year over year. The company also launched MS NOW's direct-to-consumer product and Fandango's on-demand product as new growth drivers. CNBC is expected to launch a direct-to-consumer product next year, Versant said.

For the period ended Sept. 30, Versant recorded net income of $47 million, or 34 cents per share, compared with $80 million, or 56 cents per share, the year prior.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $353 million, beating Wall Street estimates of $305 million, according to StreetAccount.

Revenue fell slightly to $1.59 billion from $1.66 billion in the year-ago period. The company said its revenue figure included the acquisition of golf simulation company Full Swing and the sale of sports management software company SportsEngine.

The company reaffirmed its full-year revenue outlook of between $6.2 billion and $6.45 billion and its adjusted EBITDA outlook of $1.9 billion to $2.05 billion. It adjusted part of its full-year guidance, saying it now expects free cash flow to exceed the prior range of between $1 billion and $1.2 billion.

"We're building on strong relationships we already have with consumers, advertisers and distributors," Lazarus said on the call. "We're finding new ways to reach and serve those audiences. We have the brands, the financial strength and the people to execute this transition successfully."

Versant began trading as a standalone company on the Nasdaq in January after separating from Comcast. Though it began largely as a portfolio of cable networks, Versant executives have said they intend to diversify the company's revenue mix to eventually draw 50% from digital, platform, subscription, ad-supported and transactional businesses.

Live sports continues to be a major driver for the company. Versant said it saw "robust audience engagement" in the third quarter and that WNBA viewership on Versant's USA Sports networks was 25% higher than the prior year's cable average.

Versant also announced an additional $100 million accelerated stock repurchase program beginning Wednesday. It expects that transaction to be completed during the fourth quarter.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#150. Google responds to political backlash over removing search from schools that ban AI

Google is playing defense on Tuesday after a political backlash to its new policy surrounding use of artificial intelligence in schools.

In a blog post titled "Designing responsible AI for learning," Google said it's bringing AI into its Classroom offering with a product called Guided Learning. The New York Times published a story saying that the company will turn off search for schools that reject AI entirely.

Following the Times' story, prominent left-leaning politicians, including Sen. Bernie Sanders from Vermont and New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani voiced their outrage on social media.

"Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools," Mamdani wrote on X. Sanders wrote that "Google is threatening schools," referring to the policy as "extortion."

Google responded later in the day. In a post on X, the company said its new policy was meant to give schools more choice over how students interact with AI. Google said its Gemini chatbot, Gemini Notebook and search are turned off by default, and that "schools are in control," with the ability to turn them as they see fit.

"We are also updating Search for schools to make it better for learning," Google wrote. "Specifically, AI Mode and AI Overviews will offer step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don't get quick answers to schoolwork."

The company's post didn't explicitly respond to the claim that access to search requires use of AI. However, Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

A Google spokesperson referred CNBC to the X post when asked for comment.

Google's broader practices have come under fire in recent years, with the company losing antitrust cases over its search and advertising businesses. Former Federal Trade Commission Chair Lina Khan aggressively pursued Google and other tech giants, including Meta and Amazon.

Khan said in a post on X on Tuesday that Google's new AI for learning policy highlights "the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service."

It's the latest controversy related to the impact of technology on children and the role leading internet companies are playing in delivering information.

In August, Meta reached a landmark $16.7 billion settlement in its child safety trial after a number of state attorneys general accused the company of misrepresenting the extent of child mental health harms caused by apps like Facebook and Instagram.

The same month, OpenAI launched ChatGPT for Teens after state attorneys general launched an investigation into the company's safety practices, with Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier, a Republican, filing a lawsuit in June.

The nonprofit Common Sense Media recently gave Google's AI overviews an "unacceptable risk" rating. Last month, Sens. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., and Josh Hawley, R-Mo., launched an inquiry over Google's push of AI into classrooms.

Google introduced AI overviews as a search function in May 2024.

— CNBC's MacKenzie Sigalos contributed to this report.

#149. Ryanair says it may freeze workers' pay and raise airfares

Ryanair is considering a temporary wage freeze for staff and confirmed airfares will be "materially higher" next summer because of a sharp rise in fuel costs following the US-Israel war with Iran.

The airline said it expects fuel prices to remain high "well into 2027" unless there is a "swift resolution" to the conflict.

Ryanair said its fuel bill is forecast to be â¬2bn (Â£1.6bn) higher in the short-term and its boss, Michael O'Leary, said airfares would have to rise by "double-digits" to help cover the cost.

But the firm said that was unlikely to be enough and it is looking into pay freezes. Unite, the union representing Ryanair staff, said it had not been told of "any such proposals".

O'Leary also said the company would slow down hiring as airline's costs rise.

The airline said its pre-tax profits fell by 16% to â¬2.4bn during the six months to the end of September, largely due to fuel costs jumping by 15%.

The company said that although 80% of its fuel bill had been "hedged" â in effect, bought in advance at fixed prices â the cost of the remainder was elevated.

O'Leary reiterated forecasts from earlier this year Ryanair would "need a 20% fare increase" to cover the cost of jet fuel.

"I'm not sure we'll get to 20%," he said. "But we're certainly looking at double-digit fare increases across Europe from all airlines in 2027."

Aviation fuel prices have surged this year as a result of the US-Israel war with Iran, which has dramatically reduced the supply of refined products coming from the Gulf and increased competition for those produced elsewhere.

Ryanair also said it was weighing up whether to freeze pay, as multi-year wage deals with unions come to to an end. The policy could be implemented from April next year.

Oliver Richardson, national officer at Unite, said the union "hasn't been advised of any such proposals and we have a multi-year pay deal in place which covers next year. A number of Unions across Europe have the same".

He said: "Our pay agreement was not attached to, nor dependent on company performance. This was something that the Ryanair was at pains to emphasise. As such we would expect it to be honoured."

Ryanair also said it would increase its use of artificial intelligence within its back office and support functions.

Meanwhile, Ryanair has cut back on winter flights and said on Monday there would be more to come. It said this was happening across the airline industry, particularly with short-haul carriers.

O'Leary said: "We believe those cuts will continue into the summer of 2027. Over the medium term, we expect short-haul capacity will remain constrained out to the early 2030s."

#147. Christmas 2026 stamps unveiled by Royal Mail

Two stamps from the Royal Mail's annual Christmas collection

Royal Mail has unveiled its Christmas stamps for this year - featuring scenes from the Nativity.

The series of five stamps have been jointly created by graphic designer Gareth Howat and illustrator Rebecca Sutherland.

The stamps depict familiar scenes of Mary and Joseph travelling, baby Jesus, an angel, the shepherds and the Three Wise Men.

This year's collection marks the 60th anniversary of the first-ever Christmas special stamp.

Sutherland said it was an "absolute honour" to be given the opportunity to illustrate the Christmas stamps for the Royal Mail.

She said she has loved stamps since she was a child and had even collected them. "Being asked to create a set myself feels incredibly special," she added.

The Christmas stamps from recent years have featured snowy scenes of UK cathedrals, or lyrics from famous carols - although 2025 also showed scenes from the Nativity.

The first Christmas stamp collection came out in December 1966 after a children's art competition was announced by the then-postmaster Tony Benn.

David Gold, of Royal Mail, said: "We hope this year's Christmas stamps will add an extra touch of festive magic to the millions of greetings sent to family and friends at home and around the world."

#145. Apple unsure when it will get more foldable iPhones ahead of Christmas

Apple has said it does not know when it will get more of its new foldable iPhones back in stock as the company heads towards the key Christmas trading period.

The iPhone Duo sold out just over a week after its launch, despite being Apple's most expensive smartphone at a cost of nearly $2,000.

The company warned of "constraints" in sourcing key components and admitted it was "really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance".

Meanwhile, Apple's new chief executive John Ternus said the firm was seeing an "unprecedented" price rise in memory costs, including chips, as the artificial intelligence (AI) industry continues to invest billions of dollars in building data centres.

Ternus launched the Duo after he took over from previous boss Tim Cook in September.

It is the smartphone's first major design change in almost 20 years but some buyers have reported problems with the Duo's screen.

Last week, the company said it would replace any handsets that were affected by an issue that caused a "small number" to crack along the inside fold.

As it released its latest financial figures on Monday, Apple's executives did not mention any problems with the iPhone Duo.

Ternus said he was "thrilled" by the early reaction to the foldable phone. Chief operating officer Sabih Khan said the company was working hard to get the Duo "into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible".

Apple's results for the three months to 26 September did not include sales of the Duo, which was released at the end of October.

Nevertheless, Apple said it had sold more iPhones globally over the last year than ever before.

iPhone revenue for the quarter rose 17% to $57bn (Â£42bn).

Sales of Mac computers also grew over the three months by 27% to a record $10.7bn.

Overall, Apple's sales between July and September rose from $102bn to $114bn. Pre-tax profit also increased, from $32.8bn to $36.1bn.

However, sales for both the iPad and Apple's wearable products, including its headphones and watch, fell by 10% and 7%, respectively.

Khan said that most Apple devices were still experiencing supply constraints driven in part by difficulty in getting enough microprocessors with advanced nodes.

Those components are essentially computer chip technology that allow Apple devices to operate more quickly, most of which it has manufactured through TSMC, which is based in Taiwan.

Khan said the problem was expected to continue into next year, particularly impacting iPads and Macs.

The situation has been made worse by what the company has repeatedly insisted is "unexpected demand" for its products.

Ternus noted, too, that Apple is still seeing "unprecedented" cost increases for memory, or the ability of devices to store data like apps, photos and text messages.

Much memory storage runs through data centres, which have experienced a boom in demand for space as AI chatbots and platforms - including those developed by the likes of OpenAI and Anthropic - have become popular.

Watch: Would you buy Apple's $2,000 foldable phone?

Storage prices have gone up for tech companies like Apple.

Earlier this year, Cook referred to a sudden increase in data centre costs as a "thousand year flood".

Apple has lifted prices on nearly all of its hardware products by around 20%.

Sunil Krishnan from Aviva Investors told the BBC's Today programme that Apple posted "a reasonably solid set of results" with a "pretty good performance" from its iPhone business.

While there were concerns over the company's guidance on profit margins and the cost of memory chips, Krishnan added that in the broader context, "margins well north of 45% for a very well-established company, I think, are pretty impressive".

However, Apple's share price fell by 1.8% in after-hours trading.

#144. North Korea denies being behind record $6.3bn Binance hack

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un

North Korea has denied claims that it was behind the record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of cryptocurrency exchange Binance, calling the allegations "absurd rhetoric".

Kim Yo Jong, a top Democratic People's Republic of Korea official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said on Tuesday that the accusations are another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage Pyongyang's public image.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), the Secret Service and cyber analysts have said North Korea orchestrated the hack, which has been described as the largest in history.

This is the latest hack to be linked to North Korea, which has been accused of relying on cyber-crime to help finance its sanctioned economy.

Blockchain analysts and the FBI have said the attack was led by state-backed hacking group TraderTraitor.

The FBI has called on industry partners to help block transactions involving cryptocurrency accounts linked to the group.

"The US has made a trite provocation of linking it with the Democratic People's Republic of Korea again," Kim Yo-jong, the director of North Korea's General Affairs Department, said in a statement.

"It is a grave political provocation against the DPRK that the US misleads the international public opinion as if all the cyber crimes in different parts of the world are our deeds," she said.

Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

But Pyongyang has been blamed for a number of major cyber attacks in recent years - accusations that it has denied.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Pyongyang has systematically built its cyber operations, recruiting promising students from universities and sending them to hacker units overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, research groups like the S Rajaratnam School of International Studies think tank have said.

The country has consistently denied such claims.

Kim Yo Jong said "it is the consistent policy stand of the DPRK government to resolutely oppose all sorts of malicious acts in cyberspace".

Kim Yo-jong: North Korea's heir apparent?

Who is Kim Yo-jong? Video, 00:02:32Who is Kim Yo-jong?

Binance did not respond to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

Since the incident, the crypto giant has largely focused its public statements on the technical nature of the breach and its response to protect users.

The company's spokesperson said that a net $4.72bn has been withdrawn from the exchange since transactions resumed on Sunday.

The net outflow represents a "relatively limited portion" of Binance's total assets, which stand at approximately $157bn, the spokesperson told the BBC on Tuesday.

That figure reflects the impact of the hack, subsequent withdrawals and market price changes.

"Withdrawal levels are stabilising to what we would consider normal and expected on-chain activity," she added.

The company said last week that initial checks indicated that an engineer's laptop had been compromised, enabling an attacker to make unauthorised withdrawals.

Trading in major cryptocurrencies on Binance has since resumed after the exchange worked with issuers to freeze assets impacted by the hack.

The company has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack and that it has so far covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach.

#143. Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

Taco Bell's same-store sales are up about 1% so far in the fourth quarter, CFO Ranjith Roy said on the company's earnings conference call Tuesday. In the last four weeks ended Friday, same-store sales have risen 3% compared with the year-ago period.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Yum reported fiscal third-quarter net income of $1.62 billion, or $5.92 per share, up from $397 million, or $1.41 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding gains from its divestiture of Pizza Hut and other items, the restaurant company earned $1.56 per share.

During the quarter, Yum completed its divestiture of Pizza Hut. It closed the sale of the pizza chain's business in mainland China to Yum China on Aug. 7 and sold the rest of Pizza Hut to LongRange Capital on Sept. 1. The company's third-quarter results include Pizza Hut up until those dates.

Excluding Pizza Hut, Yum's same-store sales were flat for the quarter as Taco Bell weighed on the company's overall results. Taco Bell's same-store sales declined by 4% as diners temporarily pulled back on visits due to the foodborne illness outbreak.

Yum executives previously said the weekend of July 18 saw the most severe impact to sales, but trends have improved since then.

Taco Bell's most loyal customers returned faster, Yum said, thanks in part to weekly Tuesday deals for loyalty program members after the cyclospora outbreak took over headlines.

Traffic from "medium" and "light" customers has bounced back to about 95% of prior levels, while visits from "heavy" users is back to normal, Roy said. To attract more casual diners, Taco Bell has been relying on star power, like a collaboration with pop star Olivia Rodrigo on a pink-colored Mountain Dew Baja Blast.

Outside of the U.S., Taco Bell's much smaller international business reported same-store sales growth of 5%.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers. Its home market represents only about 5% of KFC's operating profit, but it remains a symbolically important part of the business for Yum investors.

During the quarter, KFC U.S. launched popcorn chicken, which sold out in many restaurants. However, Yum said the limited-time menu item was not enough to offset the successful Hot & Spicy Wings and Potato Wedges promotion during the year-ago period.

Globally, KFC is trying to become "the most modern chicken QSR," Turner said on the company's earnings conference call. To achieve that lofty goal, he said the chain will roll out buzzy promotions, a new brand identity, and a wider menu that includes upgraded sauces and more beverages.

#141. Novo shares jump as Wegovy pill sales impress, drugmaker hikes outlook

Novo posted quarterly earnings ahead of expectations early Wednesday as sales of its Wegovy pill blew past estimates amid continued GLP-1 volume growth globally, sending shares up as much as 6% in early trading.

The company updated its outlook to now see adjusted revenue and operating profit growth between negative 2% and 2% at constant exchange rates. It previously saw full-year sales and operating profits, which it hiked in August, at negative 6% to flat for both metrics.

Sales in the third quarter came in at 79.2 billion Danish kroner ($11.9 billion), up 4% year-on-year at constant exchange rates and ahead of FactSet estimates of 73.6 billion kroner. Adjusted operating profit (EBIT) was 31.7 billion kroner, compared with the expected 28.6 billion kroner.

Novo saw "strong momentum" in the U.S., CEO Mike Doustdar told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday. "We today continue to see 80% market share with our product, despite the fact that competition has come and strengthened their presence," he said.

It comes as the Danish weight-loss drug maker is looking to regain momentum following a tumultuous year in which it fell behind rival Eli Lilly.

While Novo pioneered the GLP-1 weight-loss drugs now used by around 10% of Americans, it has struggled to keep the momentum going as chief rival Eli Lilly quickly caught up and surpassed Novo in their by far biggest market, the U.S. Several other pharma companies are also eyeing entry into the lucrative market.

Investors are now laser-focused on the pair's next-generation weight-loss drugs, and whether they can treat obesity-linked diseases such as heart and kidney conditions, sleep apnea and arthritis.

Novo is preparing to launch CagriSema, a combination of the GLP-1 receptor agonist semaglutide and the long-acting amylin analogue cagrilintide. Despite what Novo has touted as CagriSema's superior benefits over rival treatments, analysts have repeatedly questioned its commercial viability after the drug failed to beat Lilly's medicines that are already available.

Following the results, Citi analysts maintained their neutral rating on Novo stock, citing continued uncertainty over the near-, mid-, and long-term weight-loss drug pricing and a lack of a clear Wegovy successor in the pipeline.

Novo said sales of its Wegovy pill, launched earlier this year, totaled 5.4 billion kroner, compared with the roughly 4.5 billion kroner analysts expected.

Sales of injectable Wegovy and diabetes treatment Ozempic were 20.4 billion kroner and 29.7 billion kroner, respectively, also above expectations.

Novo also announced that Chief Financial Officer Karsten Munk Knudsen is leaving at the end of 2026 after 27 years at the company, including nine years as CFO. Luigi La Corte, currently CFO of Swiss dermatology company Galderma, will succeed him.

Novo's Wednesday earnings report comes after Lilly hiked its full-year outlook last week on the back of a better-than-expected third quarter, driven by strong demand for its diabetes and weight-loss injections Mounjaro and Zepbound.

Lilly's weight-loss pill Foundayo, however, missed analysts' expectations in the quarter even as the company's chief financial officer said total U.S. prescriptions and the number of new Foundayo patients doubled from the previous quarter.

Novo's Copenhagen-listed shares were last seen up 3.4%, bringing year-to-date losses to about 13%. The stock fell nearly 8% in one session in September after Novo failed to reassure investors with its post-Wegovy growth strategy.

Novo CEO Mike Doustdar told reporters early Wednesday that the company's recovery was gaining traction following successive upgrades to its full-year guidance, but cautioned that a turnaround could not be established on the basis of a single strong quarter.

"Turnaround and recovery is not done based on one data point, on one quarterly good result," Doustdar said, pointing to the progressive improvement in the company's outlook throughout the year.

"That gives me confidence that we're going in the right direction, and then we'll just have to continue working hard to see the recovery in full," he added.

#140. Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack

'All this food is now useless': Piles of rubbish after shop freezers hit by cyber-attack

Dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries have been hit by a cyber-attack affecting their refrigerators and freezers.

Around 40 Delhaize supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Sabcobel, a Belgian company that manufactures some of the affected refrigerators, said the units' evaporators were switched to defrost mode, causing them to warm up, in what is believed to be the same method used in last month's incident that hit hundreds of French supermarkets.

Millions of euros worth of food had to be thrown away in October, local media reported at the time.

Freezers have had to be emptied at several ICA grocery stores in Sweden after the cyber-attack

Stephan Moelans, the CEO of Sabcobel, which operates Delhaize's systems, on Monday told the Belga news agency that the incident was a cyber-attack and that the company had been "working all night and morning to disconnect the affected units from the network".

He said since the French attack in early October, the company had been working to "protect our customers fridges against this vulnerability" but that the units needed physical modifications, not just software updates.

Most supermarkets across Europe are linked to the internet, and maintenance and troubleshooting can be done remotely.

"The simplest solution is to manage the fridges over a secure internet connection. Most of our customers switched to that after the attack in France," Moelans added.

The temperature of freezers in several shops across Europe reportedly rose to as high as 50C (122F).

It is unclear who is behind the latest apparent attack.

In the US, authorities are investigating what caused refrigeration malfunctions at a number of American military bases in August and working together with French officials to examine whether the incidents could be linked.

Both Belgium and Sweden's national cyber-security agencies said they were monitoring the incident.

The UK's National Cyber Security Centre said it was working with international partners and industry to "fully understand details of an incident". No incidents have been reported in the UK.

In Flanders, northern Belgium, a Delhaize branch in Hoeilaart told news website Het Laatste Nieuws it had lost â¬100,000 (Â£83,500) worth of produce.

The manager said that estimate did not even take into account further losses that would result from closing the shop.

Carrefour, a French-owned supermarket, said the incident had only affected 15 of its Belgian stores, adding that measures had been taken immediately to ensure all shops remained open.

A spokesperson for Colruyt Group, which operates Spar stores in Belgium under a franchise system, told Reuters news agency five shops had their fridge systems attacked.

Sweden's budget furniture retailer Ikea said refrigeration systems of its store in the western Belgian city of Mons were affected, with a spokesman quoted by Reuters as saying "we cannot rule out that additional locations may be affected".

An employee at an ICA supermarket in Sweden's capital Stockholm was pictured throwing away bags of spoiled food

In Sweden, several ICA and Coop shops were hit.

A spokesperson for ICA confirmed the incident had affected a "small number of ICA stores operating outside ICA's central network".

"ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the situation," they said.

One of the ICA branches located in SjÃ¶bo, southern Sweden, wrote on social media: "Our refrigerators and freezers have been hacked overnight and they have set the temperature to 50 degrees."

The store was closed earlier but has now reopened to customers.

Seven out of the 800 Coop stores in Sweden were also affected but the "situation is currently contained and under control", the company told the BBC.

In neighbouring Finland, refrigerator malfunctions were detected in some K-stores.

The owner of the supermarket chain Kesko was quoted by Finland's Helsingin Sanomat newspaper as saying its security experts were investigating what caused the problem.

The attack is also affecting shops in Ireland, the Netherlands and Estonia.

In Ireland, it impacted 13 SuperValu supermarkets. A spokesman for the owner of the chain, Musgrave Group, confirmed to The Irish Times that a "cyber incident" affected its refrigeration systems, but added that the impact was limited to a "specific model" of refrigerator.

A spokesperson for Danish manufacturer Danfoss told Dutch public radio channel NOS that the freezing and cooling systems of at least 17 companies in the Netherlands were attacked overnight, affecting butchers' shops and small neighbourhood stores.

A Selver supermarket in Estonia was also affected and there are reports of other affected companies in the country, according to ERR.

#138. Strand bookstore to pay nearly $1.5 million over Covid loan tied to Sen. Wyden's wife

The Strand, the iconic New York City bookstore, agreed to pay nearly $1.5 million in a civil settlement for accepting a Covid government loan that was off-limits because the store is owned by the wife of Oregon Democratic Sen. Ron Wyden, according to the U.S. Attorney's Office in Washington, D.C.

The Strand applied for the $932,350 loan in February 2021 from the Paycheck Protection Program administered by the Small Business Administration, U.S. Attorney Jeanine Pirro's office said Tuesday.

Store owner Nancy Bass Wyden certified on the loan application that "the spouse of a Member of Congress does not directly or indirectly hold a controlling interest in the applicant," Pirro's office said.

"Ms. Wyden's certification was knowingly and materially false and Strand was not eligible for the second draw loan it received," the office said.

The Strand will pay $1,482,560 to settle the case. There was no determination of liability by the store, Pirro's office said.

The Strand, in a statement, said the store made an "unintentional" mistake in applying for the loan, and said it was unaware at the time the funds were barred to businesses owned by the spouse of a member of Congress.

Bass Wyden's husband, who has served in the Senate for more than 30 years, is a staunch critic of President Donald Trump, who appointed Pirro to the post of top prosecutor in the District of Columbia.

Bass Wyden is the granddaughter of Ben Bass, founder of the Strand, whose flagship store is in Greenwich Village.

Congress authorized the forgivable PPP loans when it passed the Coronavirus Aid, Relief, and Economic Security Act in March 2020.

In December 2020, Congress provided additional funding for the PPP loan program with separate legislation, which "included a prohibition against businesses obtaining second draw PPP funds if a member of Congress or their spouse directly or indirectly held a controlling interest in the PPP loan applicant," Pirro's office said.

In a statement Tuesday, Pirro said, "The Paycheck Protection Program was created to save American jobs, not to enrich those who aren't eligible for taxpayer-funded relief, especially the wife of a U.S. Senator."

Her office noted that the False Claims Act, which the Strand was alleged to have violated, "allows the government to establish liability based on reckless disregard and permits the government to recover more than its actual losses," which is what occurred in this case.

The civil settlement resolved claims brought under a so-called qui tam lawsuit, which allows private whistleblowers to sue on behalf of the U.S. government and receive a portion of any recovery.

The qui tam action in this case was filed by a corporate entity, Blockquote Inc., in U.S. District Court in Washington. Blockquote, whose owners were not disclosed by Pirro's office, will receive a total share of about $148,256 in connection with the settlement.

The docket for Blockquote's lawsuit is sealed from public view.

The Strand, in a statement after CNBC requested comment from the store and Bass Wyden, said the restriction that the store violated was passed by Congress between the first and second rounds of the PPP.

"Unfortunately, the bookstore's accountant and the bank administering the loan were not aware of this change when the second-draw application was processed," the Strand said.

"We stand by the facts: the circumstances arose during an unprecedented and rapidly changing public health emergency, the mistake was unintentional," the store said. "We hope this matter will be viewed objectively and in its proper context, with the understanding that the application of justice should always be fair, impartial, and free from political considerations," the store said.

A spokesman for Sen. Wyden's office said the senator and his wife do not discuss her business, and that he had no involvement in the loan or settlement.

#137. 'Quiet quitting' could be hitting the economy - here's why

The so-called "quiet quitting" trend where workers do the basic requirements of a job but refuse to do anything more might be hitting the economy, analysis has found.

Over the last decade, Britons have become less engaged with the jobs they do and are less willing to put in extra effort, according to a Resolution Foundation report.

The thinktank said this could partly help explain the slump in productivity since the pandemic.

"This is self-evidently bad for workers, but it also doesn't bode well for productivity and labour supply, both of which are vital for lifting living standards in the years ahead," said Hannah Slaughter, principal economist at the Resolution Foundation.

Quiet quitting was popularised on social media shortly after the pandemic.

Frustrated by high demands, low pay, and a squeeze in living standards, many online posts said workers should only do what their bosses asked and nothing more.

So you still show up for work, but no more helping out with additional tasks or checking emails outside work hours, instead focusing on work-life balance and "acting your wage".

Yet, far from being just a talking point on TikTok, the Resolution Foundation said the findings in its report were "consistent" with the idea of quiet quitting.

'I was working 60 hours a week so I quiet quit'

The percentage of those surveyed willing to go the extra mile to help their employer has sunk from 69% in 2015 to 55% in 2025, according to their survey of 4,656 adults.

Meanwhile, those who were not even "fairly" satisfied with their roles has sunk to one-in-four, the lowest level on record.

In addition, workers are less likely than 10 years ago to say they have an interesting job, with 63% of those surveyed saying so, down from 74%.

"Workers today are indeed a little less committed to their employers, and a little less intrinsically drawn to work in general, than workers were a decade ago," the report added.

This shift over the last decade "may have contributed to weak productivity growth", which has slowed since the financial crisis and weakened again after the pandemic, the Resolution Foundation found.

Workers were also more focused on pay than in the past, which it said was "perhaps unsurprising... given the flatlining living standards of the past decade".

Sir John Curtice, politics professor and co-editor of the report, said the analysis does not mean that young people have "become more 'work-shy', nor have workers in general become more reluctant to change jobs".

"But if workers are to be fully engaged in their work, it has seemingly become more important for employers to provide jobs that are both financially rewarding and have flexible work hours," he added.

Are you quiet quitting? Tell us how and why.

#134. From scooters to shampoo - the people saving hundreds from dumpster diving

Darryl Baker was shocked to see piles of clothes dumped beside the recycling bins outside her local supermarket.

On several trips to the dumpsters she has also found a host of other items, such as "a perfectly decent" Ikea picture frame and children's scooters.

Always on the lookout for ways to save money and appalled by the waste, the thrifty mother-of-two from north London asked supermarket staff if she could take some of it.

Permission granted, "I got my children entire new wardrobes," says Darryl, 39, adding that she's stuff put away for years to come.

During a six-month stint this year, "I have probably saved hundreds of pounds," says the freelance journalist.

Darryl reels off the names of brands she has salvaged - Ralph Lauren, Nike and The North Face, among others. "My mind was blown," she says. "This stuff is just strewn all over the floor."

Darryl Baker takes the clothes for herself and her children, and to sell

In addition to finding clothing for her children and her nephews and nieces, she keeps some for herself. Others she donates or sells, including recently getting Â£30 for a men's Barbour jacket.

Darryl has joined the ranks of so-called dumpster divers â people who rummage through commercial bins for treasures. Motivations include saving money, giving items to charity and disgust at how often perfectly usable items are being consigned to waste - or a combination of all three.

Texan Jeff Ferrell has been rooting around in trash for more than 50 years.

"I still dumpster dive every day by bicycle," says the 72-year old, who lives in Fort Worth, and has written books on the topic. "I still enjoy it as much, or more, than ever."

Jeff considers himself a "bit of Robin Hood mixed with anarchist direct action", only scrounging in more affluent neighbourhoods and giving away many of his finds.

"Almost everything in our home is scrounged, and I never buy, for example, soap or toiletries or medical supplies, or shoes and clothes and tools and garden supplies," he says.

Jeff Ferrell sells some of what he finds, and gives away other items

Jeff looks for discarded metal to sell at a scrapyard which, he says, earns him around $150 (Â£110) every two weeks. His wife sells his other finds, such as "an antique or piece of jewellery", on eBay.

Through savings and selling the more valuable items, he estimates his finds are worth "tens of thousands of dollars a year".

While dumpster diving may have been considered taboo in the past, it's now something many people are proud to do and post about online.

Millie Diamond, a London-based stylist, posts videos on Instagram of her rummaging through bins. But she has a very specific aim - to unearth fabrics and clothing that she can salvage and refashion into new items, some of which she then sells.

Millie Diamond turns dumped textiles and clothes into new outfits

She estimates that one dress she found was worth Â£600.

"I'm a bit of a magpie. I like collecting things off the street or anywhere that there's a freebie. If I see something that catches my eye, then I'll grab it."

Millie, 36, began bin diving after spotting some fabrics discarded by a business that had shut down. "I literally clambered into the bin. It turned out it was loads of vintage clothes."

For Charlotte Bayford in Essex it's all about timing. Her best finds come when students at Essex University head home for the summer and ditch anything they don't want to take with them.

This includes half-filled toiletries. "I haven't bought shampoo for eight years," says the 44-year-old sales assistant. "I'd say that has saved me some money."

Like other dumpster divers, Charlotte gives a lot of her finds to charity. But she has also filled her house with salvaged objects, and sells on some valuable items.

Food, as long as it remains sealed, is fair game too. "I've found tins of Heinz baked beans, perfectly fine. I'm quite happy to eat those," Charlotte says.

Charlotte Bayford gives some of her finds to charity

But is taking things out of bins and keeping them acceptable in the eyes of the law?

Jeff, who is professor emeritus of sociology at Texas Christian University, says many US cities have made the practice illegal, including New York, Cleveland and Wichita.

"Dumpster diving is considered illegal [in such places] because that which is thrown away now belongs to the city... or to the private company that provided trash services," he explains. Any dumpster diver also risks trespass charges.

In Fort Worth, where Jeff lives, items can only be removed from bins if they are on public land, external.

In the UK, Sunjay Versani of Duncan Lewis Solicitors says it depends on the circumstances.

"In many cases involving genuinely discarded goods, prosecution is unlikely," he says.

"But there is no general rule that something is free to take simply because it has been put in a skip or bin. If in doubt, the safest approach is to ask the owner or business for permission."

#132. Mining giant joins fight to stop killer toad march across Australia

Rio Tinto is ploughing millions of dollars into stopping the poisonous cane toad reaching the Pilbara region of western Australia, where the mining giant has a vast iron ore operation.

The firm, alongside Curtin University in Perth and others, will spend A$28m (Â£14.5m) building a "toad containment zone" to prevent the pest travelling south.

While the cane toad is not a direct threat to Rio Tinto's business, it produces toxins lethal to native wildlife and poses a threat to Pilbara's ecosystem.

Rio Tinto has previously faced criticism for destroying two sacred Aboriginal rock shelters in Pilbara which sat above tonnes of iron ore.

The decision cost the former chief executive his job, provoked a widespread backlash and saw Rio Tinto commit to rebuilding trust with Indigenous communities.

Commenting on the proposed containment zone, Roberta Hunter, a ranger in Australia's Nyangumarta Warrarn Indigenous Protected Area, said: "Cane toads destroy the water and animals.

"When toads come close to our country, we want to be there to stop them because we don't want the country to be destroyed."

The plan is to cut off the toad's water supply by building a barrier across 7,500 sq km (2,895 sq m) of land between the Great Sandy Desert and the Indian Ocean.

In drier months, the cane toad needs water every few days to survive.

If there are no natural water sources, the toad relies on artificial water points normally used for cattle and irrigation.

The funding will be used to stop the toad accessing dams, storage tanks or other ground-level water sources. The five-year project will begin in 2027.

A number of native species are at risk if the toad gets into Pilbara. These include the northern quoll, whose numbers have been decreasing, the goanna lizard and the ghost bat.

The toads were introduced into Australia in 1935 to control beetles within the sugar cane industry.

The northern quoll is at risk from the cane toad in Australia

Since then, they have been on a slow march west across the country's northern landscape, according to the Australian government, travelling between 40 km and 60 km a year.

There are no predators or diseases that control the toads, which defend themselves using poison.

Ben Phillips, a professor at Curtin University, said the toads would reach Pilbara within two decades without action.

He said: "The toad containment zone is a once in a lifetime opportunity to stop one of the world's worst invasive species in its tracks and protect the region's unique wildlife."

#131. Pfizer hikes full-year outlook on new product growth, taps company veteran as new CFO

Pfizer on Tuesday reported fiscal third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares with a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC, Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla said the company's Covid product revenue was "very low" this quarter and year, but called it "positive news for us."

"Covid revenues was an overhang on the performance of our stock because of the perception that it is volatile and it's material to us. Actually, we've proven that we can make it irrelevant," Bourla said. "If anything, only an upside because, in the lowest possible Covid performance, we are beating, and we are raising by $1.5 billion because of the overperformance of the other products."

Pfizer reaffirmed its Covid product outlook of roughly $4 billion for the year, expecting the majority of Covid shot sales in the fourth quarter during vaccination season, Guegan said during the call.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, Bourla is looking to jump-start growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

"In addition to the revenues that they generate now, and they will continue generating so that we can overcome the patent, the loss of patent exclusivity, we are bringing pipeline that will perform even better in terms of growth," Bourla told CNBC, referring to Pfizer's launched and acquired products.

Pfizer in August expanded its multiyear cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares with the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products contributed $3.8 billion, growing 32% operationally, Guegan said during the earnings call. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

'We continue to invest behind this group of products to mitigate [loss of exclusivity] headwinds over the next several years," Guegan said. "In addition, this quarter's performance demonstrates clear progress against our objective of returning to growth toward the end of the decade."

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares with the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54 billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

#129. China expands overseas mining stakes, tightening grip on critical minerals

China is mobilizing state-linked companies to take bigger ownership stakes in mines overseas, seeking to strengthen its stranglehold over the metals vital for electric vehicles, semiconductors and jet engines

The state-led push could give Beijing greater leverage over global supplies of critical minerals and who gets access to them, analysts say.

"China is rolling out a state-coordinated global mining strategy" to replace firm-level decision-making, said Dan Wang, China director at Eurasia, adding that the model would allow for larger, better-aligned global mining investments.

In April, a dedicated overseas bureau was set up to oversee investments abroad by central state-owned enterprises. Several government agencies, ministries and policy banks have been mobilized to steer overseas mining deals.

Later in July, a mining investment vehicle was created to coordinate overseas deals, advising Chinese companies on compliance, risk management, and market conditions as they face growing geopolitical tensions and regulatory hurdles abroad.

The push for greater centralization could curb disorderly competition among Chinese firms and strengthen their hand against Western competitors and newer entrants, such as Saudi Arabia and India, Wang said, though a longer review and approval process could also raise corruption risks and weigh on investment returns.

Chinese firms have invested heavily in overseas mining for two decades, making big bets in places avoided by Western miners, from Tajikistan to the Democratic Republic of Congo. The push has gained urgency as governments increasingly view critical minerals as a source of geopolitical leverage.

Chinese mineral acquisitions abroad reached $8.8 billion in the first three quarters this year — already the highest annual total since at least 2018, according to a Rhodium Group report released Wednesday.

"Acquisitions have mostly been concentrated in minerals that China lacks domestically," said Armand Meyer, senior research analyst at Rhodium, naming lithium, nickel and cobalt during the electric-vehicle boom in the mid-2010s and gold in recent years.

Outbound investment in upstream critical minerals will likely roughly double from the 2021-2025 period, according to Eurasia, as China doubles down on overseas push to secure access and reserves that it lacks domestically.

Beijing treats 70% import dependence as an implicit red line – which puts copper, lithium, cobalt, nickel, bauxite and iron ore under a hard national security constraint to expand supply abroad, Wang said.

Even where reliance is lower, Beijing wants more. For gold, which sits within the 50% "safe" range, the government still seeks additional access to high-grade resources, Wang said. That pursuit of scale and "ultimately geopolitical strength" could override decisions over investment risk and profitability, she added.

But the push is facing growing resistance as host governments demand more local processing, a stage China dominates and has turned into a key supply-chain chokepoint. More resource-rich countries are restricting exports of unprocessed minerals and requiring foreign partners to refine them locally.

Countries have long shipped rare earths to China for processing because refining carries high environmental costs, allowing Beijing to build out massive processing capacity across the country. The U.S. has abundant supplies of rare earths, but it relies on China to refine them.

China now produces about 92% of the world's refined rare earths, according to the International Energy Agency.

That gives Beijing the ability to withhold supply or flood the market to influence prices and project power, said Brad Handler, director of Energy Finance Lab at the Payne Institute. "If a rival builds a new refinery, China can produce more, oversupply the market and push prices below economic levels," he said.

China has curbed exports of seven medium and heavy rare earths since last year, requiring exporters to obtain licenses.

Japan has been among the hardest hit, reportedly coming within months of running out of yttrium. The element, used for screen displays and semiconductors, is produced as a byproduct during the extraction of other rare earth metals.

China's exports of yttrium oxide to Japan collapsed to 4,000 kilograms in September from 51,000 kilograms a year earlier, according to Chinese customs data. And the oxide's price reportedly surged to about $1,150 a kilogram outside China — roughly 179 times its level two years earlier — compared with about $9 a kilogram inside the country.

Japan spent 16 years building stockpiles after China's 2010 restrictions, yet "there is virtually no yttrium or lutetium separation capacity outside of China," Bedford said.

But Chinese companies have adapted in recent years, ramping up greenfield investment and setting up processing capacity near the mines, instead of outright acquisitions, according to Rhodium.

"That is most visible where host governments require local processing," said Meyer, pointing to Indonesia's ban on nickel ore exports that has led Chinese firms to build smelting and refining capacity in the Southeast Asian country, instead of shipping them back to China.

#127. Asda hires ex-Morrisons boss in bid to grow firm

Asda headquarters are based in Leeds city centre

Asda has appointed a former Morrisons boss as chief executive to help lead revival efforts at the supermarket chain.

Asda, which is based in Leeds, said David Potts would take on the role with immediate effect, while current boss Allan Leighton would become non-executive chairman, two years after returning to lead the supermarket chain.

Potts led Morrisons from its Bradford headquarters for eight years until 2023, overseeing a turnaround during which the supermarket chain was bought by US private equity firm Clayton, Dubilier & Rice.

Potts said Asda had "made good progress but significant opportunities remain" to reinvigorate sales, with a focus on keeping prices low for shoppers.

"By making improvements to the shopping trip every week, which customers can experience and feel in their pockets, we will revitalise the business and bring benefits for all our stakeholders, including our 116,296 very hard-working colleagues," he said.

Before joining Morrisons, Potts was chief executive of Tesco's Irish business, its UK arm and its Asian operation.

David Potts led Morrisons for eight years until 2023

According to Asda, Leighton had planned to step aside from the day-to-day running of the group, after he returned in 2024, more than two decades after serving as chief executive.

"Over the last two years, we have stabilised the business and returned it to some growth," said Leighton.

"David has an outstanding record in our industry, his experience is exactly what we now require for the next stage of our transformation and beyond and it will be great to work with him."

Last year, issues with an IT upgrade hit product availability at the supermarket and dented sales, impacting its bottom line.

But in August, it reported sales had returned to growth for the current quarter for the first time in two years.
