#125. Drivers may get compensation offers before car finance saga ends

Drivers may be offered compensation from lenders ahead of the next legal battle in the car finance mis-selling saga.

Regulator the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) said it would welcome lenders making settlement offers as long as customers were not given a worse deal than under its proposed central scheme.

That scheme is facing a legal challenge, to be heard in February, with resulting compensation unlikely to be paid until late next year.

But Barclays has confirmed it will make early compensation offers from now, with others expected to follow suit.

The FCA said it would still only expect a minority of claimants to be made early offers, with most having to wait for the outcome of next year's court case.

Stephen Braviner Roman, the FCA's general counsel and co-lead on the motor finance project, said: "Fair settlement offers could result in some people getting money sooner. It's clear they want the choice.

"Anyone who wants to wait can, and there's no obligation to agree to the offer. We'll closely monitor firms making offers and expect them to be clear what accepting an offer means."

The vast majority of new cars, and many second-hand ones, are bought with finance agreements. Customers pay an initial deposit to secure the vehicle, then a monthly fee with interest.

But millions of drivers who took out a car loan between April 2007 and November 2024 will be entitled to compensation after the loans were mis-sold.

They mostly related to when car dealers received commission from lenders, based on the interest rate charged to the customer. These were known as discretionary commission arrangements (DCAs) and customers were often not told about them.

Average compensation payouts of about Â£829 are expected to be made related to about 12 million car loans - about 37% of the total number during the period.

Car finance compensation payments delayed until next year

Compensation will cost the industry up to Â£9.1bn, including administrative costs. Individual payouts will vary, depending on the degree of harm each driver faced.

With huge compensation payouts inevitable, some lenders have already set aside millions of pounds to cover the cost.

Barclays has decided to start making compensation payments early, potentially to start clearing the cost off its balance sheet. It pointed out that claimants had already faced "a considerable wait".

"By making these early settlement offers, we are giving some customers the option of receiving redress sooner, rather than waiting for the legal challenges and any subsequent FCA action to conclude," said a Barclays Partner Finance spokesperson.

"Each offer will be calculated using the FCA's redress scheme methodology. We will clearly explain how it has been calculated and what accepting a full and final settlement means, so customers can decide whether accepting the offer is right for them."

The lender said claimants would start to be contacted directly "in the coming weeks".

But it stressed not all customers whose complaints were acknowledged on or before 1 October 2026 would receive an early settlement offer.

The FCA has published a factsheet to help drivers, external work out whether they should accept early settlements from lenders.

It will include a list of lenders who are making early offers, to ensure claimants with other lenders are not scammed.

The regulator said lenders will need to be clear with people about what accepting any offer means. This includes:

Being able to keep any payment, even if the final settlement under the central scheme after the legal challenges proved to be smaller

Claimants would not be able to claim more later if the outcome of the legal challenge meant they would have been paid more

Accepting an offer takes you out of the scheme and stops any further input from the financial ombudsman

The Finance and Leasing Association, which represents lenders, said: "We want to see consumers receive the fairest outcome in as timely a way as possible and recognise that, in some cases, that may point to early settlement, while in others it may be appropriate to await greater legal certainty."

But Kevin Durkin, solicitor advocate and director at HD Law, who represented Marcus Johnson who won his case in the Supreme Court, said he had "real reservations" that fair compensation would be offered.

"These are only heightened by reports that just a small number of claimants will receive what could be lowball offers. I fear this is crumbs from the table, for some customers who are highly likely to be owed more, while the vast majority will receive no offer at all," he said.

The main proposed FCA scheme remains partially suspended, pending legal challenges in February.

Consumer group Consumer Voice said the proposed compensation scheme left "too many people short-changed". Three lenders - Volkswagen Financial Services, Mercedes Benz Financial Services, and Credit Agricole Auto Finance - are also challenging the scheme.

The UK's Upper Tribunal has agreed to hear legal challenges to the scheme, with a hearing scheduled from 16 February to 3 March.

Anyone who is concerned about their car finance deal and has not yet complained can still complain directly to their lender. There is information on the FCA website on how to complain for free, external.

#124. Mortgage rates drop for the third straight week, but homebuyers are still leaving the market

After a brief bounce the week before, mortgage demand continued its slide last week, despite falling mortgage rates. Total application volume dropped 1.2% week to week, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association's seasonally adjusted index.

The average contract interest rate for 30-year fixed-rate mortgages with conforming loan balances, $832,750 or less, decreased last week to 7.3% from 7.37%, with points declining to 0.75 from 0.76, including the origination fee, for loans with a 20% down payment.

"Mortgage rates drifted down to their lowest level since September 2026 last week in the wake of the Federal Reserve's decision to hold on additional rate hikes at its October meeting," said Mike Fratantoni, senior vice president and chief economist at the MBA.

Refinance demand was essentially flat for the week, rising just 0.1%. It was, however, 60% lower than the same week one year ago. Mortgage rates, despite falling for now three straight weeks, are still about a full percentage point higher than they were at this time last year. That gives borrowers very little incentive to refinance.

CNBC's Property Play with Diana Olick covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, delivered weekly to your inbox.

Applications for a mortgage to purchase a home fell 2% from the previous week and were 19% lower year over year. The housing market shows no signs of thawing, after the sharp rate increase in September sent it into a deep freeze.

The share of adjustable-rate mortgages, which offer lower rates but carry the risk of eventually resetting higher, continued to climb. The average rate on a 5/1 ARM dropped to 6.29% from 6.41% the previous week.

"As we have highlighted in recent weeks, more borrowers are choosing ARMs in this environment to lower their initial payments, with the ARM share at its highest level since September 2025," added Fratantoni.

Mortgage rates moved slightly higher to start this week, according to a separate survey by Mortgage News Daily. That came after a hotter-than-expected report on job openings.

"All else equal, stronger labor market data tends to put upward pressure on rates. Tech stocks were also modestly higher [Tuesday], and weakness in the tech sector has been a key factor driving persistently lower rates over the past several weeks," wrote Matthew Graham, chief operating officer at Mortgage News Daily.

Correction: Total application volume dropped 1.2% week to week, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association's seasonally adjusted index. An earlier version misstated the figure.

#122. Graduates are managing AI - but are they learning much else?

Managing AI agents is a big part of Lotte Underwood's job

Recent university graduate Lotte Underwood is a public relations executive with a twist - she spends more time using AI than actually doing PR.

Underwood, 25, joined London-based PR agency Fight or Flight in November last year, in the new role of "AI associate" after completing a degree and then a masters in marketing.

She's part of a recently established, dedicated AI team that has built around 30 AI agents. Specific tasks include industry research, client reporting, media interview preparation, helping clients create clearer briefs, and image generation.

A big part of Underwood's job is to manage those agents, check their outputs and feed those to the team members who are dealing directly with clients and the media.

Another key responsibility of hers is to monitor how clients appear in the responses from AI tools like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and others.

"A huge part is being in client meetings and calls to work out what is actually going to be most helpful from these agents," says Underwood.

AI is seen as one of the main factors behind graduate job cuts, but companies are also creating dedicated AI-specific graduate roles like Underwood's.

Dillan Patel, for example, joined engineering software company Stride 18 months ago as an intern. A business management graduate, he was put to work developing an internal chatbot designed to capture the knowledge of a senior staff member transitioning into retirement.

Patel, 24, is now an AI automation analyst, tasked with developing further internal agents to provide specific information to individual teams, while also identifying which manual or repetitive tasks across the company could be done by AI.

"My role focuses on finding practical ways to use technology, automation, data and AI to improve how we operate," says Coventry-based Patel.

Debra Maxwell is looking for "AI disruptors"

Such AI savvy graduates are also in demand from companies like ArvatoConnect, an outsourced contact centre company.

CEO Debra Maxwell says the company's usual graduate intake is on hold this year, but she plans to resume it next year with a bigger focus on AI.

"We would be looking for more AI-disruptor type roles, where you can challenge us as an organisation to think more holistically about where AI can be applied and where we could grow our business from," says London-based Maxwell.

"We see our graduates as not filling administrative roles, but actually adding value to the organisation in a different way."

Maxwell adds: "For example, one of our recent graduates is using AI to do deep dives on specific markets, and provide more information for when we're bidding [for new contracts]. It's basically giving him a more rounded role."

If AI can make a graduate job bigger and more integral to the organisation, Maxwell argues that companies can create a more loyal, engaged workforce that is able to contribute more meaningfully, much earlier.

Research from consultancy PwC found that some graduate roles are assuming more responsibilities because of AI, as it allows companies to demand more senior abilities from junior hires.

For the consultancy's 2026 AI Jobs Barometer, external, it analysed 2.4 million entry-level jobs in the US. It found that the entry-level roles most influenced by AI are now seven times more likely to require traditionally senior-level skills. These include motivational leadership, strategic decision making and team building.

Those entry-level roles with higher expectations have in fact grown 35% since 2019, PwC found, while other entry-level roles actually shrank by 10%.

Cian Clinton uses around ten AI agents for work tasks

Cian Clinton, who joined consultancy Valliance last September after graduating from a Bachelor of Science in 2024, says the incorporation of AI into his role makes him feel less like a graduate, and more like an equal team member. He uses around 10 AI agents daily for different tasks.

"Your ideas and your creativity are a lot more valued because you actually get to implement those firsthand, and the responsibility you take on is a lot greater because of that," says London-based Clinton, 23.

"I am now taking on responsibilities which would usually take me four or five years to get to."

His career progression, he believes, will now be faster as a result.

"AI is killing the 'pyramid' approachâ¦progression is no longer based on your knowledge bank. Now it's more based on your critical thinking and understanding of the processes," says Clinton.

There's no doubt Underwood, Patel and Clinton are developing valuable AI knowledge which can help them across their careers.

But with their time at work so focused on AI, does it matter how much - or how little - time they're spending learning about the professional field they're supposed to be operating in?

Underwood says she'd like to still pick up the traditional PR skills her role isn't currently focused on, but is confident of her future in the agency's growing AI team.

"There's certain things that I haven't had the chance to do yet, like directly pitching to the media. I absolutely plan to do this," she says.

Graduates becoming too AI-centric is an issue for Simon Pratt, chief operating officer at Portman Finance Group. He says being able to use AI effectively is important, but the company's two-year graduate programme is fully centred on "learning the trade".

"Our graduates rotate across four areas at six-month intervals, working directly alongside senior relationship managers to learn their trade," Northampton-based Pratt explains.

"They'll learn all of our financial products, how to draft credit papers for submission to lenders, and how to build meaningful relationships with our customers. They'll progress to running transactions from start to finish."

Claire Reid, PwC UK's chief technology and innovation officer, believes graduate roles that are too AI heavy won't necessarily provide graduates long-term career progression.

"There will be no graduate job now that doesn't require a baseline of AI literacy," says Glasgow-based Reid.

"But AI skills on their own are not going to help you in your career. You've got to couple that with that sector knowledge of what that client actually wants."

Back in Coventry, Dillan Patel is confident his AI work will continue to be valuable, and he wants to keep building on it.

"I don't think I'd want to explore different positions within the business. Through my involvement in AI-related projects, I already work closely with different areas of the business and I'm gaining valuable knowledge and experience along the way," he says.

Why are more roofs not made of solar tiles?

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

The virtual worlds where robots are trained

#120. Canva announces major job cuts after 'our hardest year yet'

Canva co-founder Melanie Perkins says this year has been "our hardest" yet

Canva, one of Australia's best known start-ups, has announced a shake up of the business that will affect 22% of its workers, including job cuts.

In what was "without doubt the hardest moment in our thirteen-year history" co-founder and CEO Melanie Perkins said 1,271 people would either lose their jobs, be asked to relocate or change roles.

The firm's value has reportedly dropped in the past year over concerns about the impact of artificial intelligence (AI) on its business as well as the cost of incorporating the technology into its platforms.

It had been "our hardest year yet, and as we shared earlier this year, our growth has slowed for the first time in a decade," Perkins wrote to staff.

The "incredibly hard decision" meant "significant changes," Perkins said.

"For some people, this will mean changes to their roles or being asked to relocate to centralised hubs. Sadly, it will also mean saying goodbye to teammates and friends who have helped build Canva into what it is today."

The company, which has staff in 37 countries, said Sydney would become the "primary home" for more of its product teams and that as of next year hybrid workers would be expected to spend two to three days in the office.

Earlier this year two of its backers, Blackbird Ventures and Airtree, reportedly downgraded its valuation from A$60bn ($41.8bn; Â£31.1bn) at the end of last year to about A$49bn.

Canva also reportedly cut its own internal valuation amid soaring AI expenses related to the roll out of new products.

"New technology is transforming what people can create, how they create it, and what they expect from the products they use," Perkins said in her note on Wednesday.

Perkins, who left university at 19 to found her first venture, launched Canva in 2013.

The company offers a variety of templates and tools to help users without technical skills or a design background to create a range of professional-looking graphics and presentations, from wedding invitations to business pitch decks.

#118. Binance says fake job interviews used as part of $6.3bn crypto hack

Hackers who stole a record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) from Binance used fake job interviews and a compromised internal AI tool, the firm has revealed.

Initial findings published by the company, which operates the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, said hackers gained access to its systems through a "sophisticated, multi-stage campaign".

It said they used a range of techniques such as malicious software downloads to compromise the computers of Binance engineers and gain control over tools to drain its wallet of billions of dollars worth of crypto.

Binance called it a "serious and highly sophisticated security incident" and apologised, saying "this should not have happened".

In a post on X, external, Binance said hackers targeted its engineers using social engineering techniques.

These see hackers use approaches such as phishing to trick people into parting with sensitive or personal details, which can then be used to try and access their accounts.

Binance said as part of the hack, one of its engineers was contacted through a legitimate job-hunting platform and invited to bogus job interviews with fake company representatives.

It said the perpetrators used "more advanced and deceptive" methods to build trust with him, such as sharing social media accounts which contained personal images.

The engineer in question was instructed to download malware masquerading as an "assignment" during a second interview, which deployed malicious code with "obfuscation techniques" evading detection.

Binance said hackers then leveraged the engineer's compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain control over two other engineers' laptops, and execute the next steps of the attack.

The incident led the firm to suspend withdrawals for customers globally, meaning users could not get their cryptocurrency out of the platform until the service was restored on Sunday.

Customers withdrew billions of dollars worth of assets after it reopened.

"We apologise for the concern, disruption and uncertainty it caused our users, partners and the wider industry," it said.

The company added it had taken a range of steps following the incident, including working with law enforcement to trace and freeze affected assets.

It said it had also set aside more than 100% of the funds needed to cover the loss, and said 98% of the token assets impacted had been "replenished" as of Saturday.

It is thought to be the largest crypto hack to date.

What we know about the $6.3bn Binance hack

US crime agencies, including the FBI and Secret Service, have been assisting Binance in tracing the crypto taken from the platform's own wallets.

The agencies, along with a range of cyber analysts, said the hack - like similar breaches before it - bore the signs of having been orchestrated by North Korea.

Pyongyang hit back at the claims on Tuesday, denying it was behind the hack.

Kim Yo Jong, a top North Korean official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said the accusations were another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage the counry's public image.

The country has consistently denied being behind a number of major cyber-attacks it has been blamed for in recent years.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Binance had previously not responded to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

But on Tuesday, the company wrote in its post on X, external: "The latest information released by the Federal Bureau of Investigation indicates that the Democratic People's Republic of Korea is responsible for this incident."

As part of its security efforts following the attack, Binance said it would also increase investment in AI tools which can detect security threats, as well as engage third-party cyber experts to carry out a full-scale review of the incident.

It also said that it would seek to establish a security research fund and work with leading AI and security firms on targeted initiatives.

#115. Disney plans several thousand layoffs, asks some managers to cut costs by up to 20%

Disney is planning to cut several thousand jobs, people familiar with the matter told CNBC.

Some managers at the entertainment giant have been asked to reduce costs by up to 20%, according to the people, who asked not to be named because they were not authorized to speak publicly.

It marks the latest round of layoffs since CEO Josh D'Amaro took the reins earlier this year. The update comes after separate plans in April to cut as many as 1,000 employees, predominantly in the marketing department, and in September to cut about 300 employees, many in human resources and technology divisions.

Bloomberg was first to report the latest round of layoffs.

Company executives warned of the possibility of more reductions in its August earnings report.

"We remain highly focused on reducing costs across the enterprise to create incremental capacity to invest for growth and are evaluating a variety of levers," D'Amaro and CFO Hugh Johnston wrote in an Aug. 5 letter to shareholders. "We are mid-stream in this work and will provide future updates on our progress."

Disney introduced plans in August for an early retirement program for some employees.

"This is one of several actions we're taking to reshape our organization, including involuntary staff reductions that have already begun in some areas and will continue into next year," Chief People Officer Sonia Coleman wrote in a letter to employees on Aug. 24.

Disney had about 231,000 employees as of the close of its fiscal year 2025, which ended in September of that year, according to the company's annual financial report.

Disney is not the only entertainment company looking to reduce costs. Netflix said in a memo last month that it would lay off roughly 6% of its workforce. Skydance, the newly combined company of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery, is also expected to cut staff in an effort to realize $6 billion in synergies over three years.

Disney will report its fiscal fourth-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

#114. Uproar over missing 'pop' as Rice Krispies changes its recipe

Rice Krispies and Coco Pops, long-standing staples of the family breakfast table, will no longer be made of whole rice grains in the UK, after Kellogg's said it was changing its recipe.

Instead the tiny toasted puffs will be made using corn flour and rice flour, and will also now contain wheat gluten as an ingredient.

A Kellogg's spokesperson said the reformulation was due to "changing weather conditions" that have disrupted supplies of some grains and cereals.

The cereal giant said the new versions had been "carefully assessed for taste, appearance and texture", but fans complained online they no longer made the famous "pop" sound and said their children would no longer eat them.

Some expressed dismay that the taste and texture were "totally different" and dubbed the change "grim" and "disgusting".

"There's no snap, crackle and pop in these," a parent named Dani wrote on TikTok. "My toddler won't eat them."

Lauren, also posting on TikTok, added that she had been eating Rice Krispies every day for breakfast "for years and years".

"I had a bowl of the new recipe and you can immediately tell the difference," she said. "They don't have the snapping sound when you put in the milk."

Jessicake meanwhile said they tasted "weird and stale".

The "Snap, Crackle and Pop" - made when milk is added to the puffed rice - has been a centrepiece of the brand's marketing since the 1950s.

Recipe changes are notoriously difficult for big brands. Chocolate makers are constantly accused of reducing cocoa content in their products and debates rage constantly online over whether ketchup recipes have changed.

When Scottish fizzy drink Irn Bru cut its sugar content in 2018, fans famously stockpiled crates of the old version.

No changes are being made to Rice Krispies products in the US and Canada.

Kellog's said the recipe change in the UK will help "maintain consistent quality" as extreme weather takes a toll on crops and pushes up ingredient prices.

Rice Krispies, launched nearly a century ago, are sold around the world and come in variations such as vanilla and strawberry flavours in some markets alongside the widespread chocolate-flavoured Coco Pops version.

"We appreciate that even small changes to a much-loved product can feel noticeable and that's why we have approached this with a lot of care and consideration," Kellogg's said.

The cereals have already undergone some change, with reductions in salt content in the 1990s and lowered sugar in 2018. The company said Rice Krispies and Coco Pops will continue to meet UK food standards on levels of fat, salt and sugar in food.

A backlash against processed foods in recent years, especially those low in fibre and high in sugar, has put cereals generally under the spotlight from healthy eating advocates.

Kellogg's stressed the cereals contain no artificial colours, flavours or sweeteners and the nutritional benefits in each bowl, such as fibre, Vitamin D and iron are staying the same.

However, the new recipes mean packaging will now include a "contains wheat gluten" warning.

Previously packaging said the products "may contain gluten" because they were made in a factory handling other cereals and there was a risk of cross-contamination.

Watch: How does El NinÌo affect world weather?

Kellogg's said it has not changed the recipe for any of its other cereals.

Most of the world's rice comes from Asia, many parts of which has been affected by droughts, floods and heatwaves due to the El NiÃ±o weather phenomenon.

The weather event is expected to severely impact crop production from South East Asia in particular. The region produces more than a quarter of the world's rice.

The World Meteorological Agency expects the record-breaking El NiÃ±o will last until at least 2027.

The company separated into two distinct firms in 2023.

The largest is Kellanova, which focuses on snacks, cereals, noodles and other food products like Eggo, Pringles and Pop-Tarts sold globally. It is now owned by Mars, which makes confectionery and pet food.

The other business, WK Kellogg's, operates in North America and has since been taken over by Italian chocolate giant Ferrero.

#112. Americans have said 'NO TO WAR,' Iran speaker says as Democrats take Congress

Iran's speaker of parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said Americans have said "NO TO WAR," as projections from U.S. midterm elections showed that Democrats have flipped both houses of Congress.

Posting on X after projections showed that Democrats will have a majority in the House and Senate over the coming two years, Ghalibaf said that "Conscience or the cost of living, Americans said NO TO WAR, and we endorse this message."

"If the [U.S. government] betrays their public will to serve international paymasters and blackmailers, it will learn our NO comes with a Nightmare."

The X account of Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei posted: "The process of the US's collapse has accelerated."

"If there are still those who entertain the slightest hope that the hegemonic system may change behavior or shifts in its political parties may bring about change, or that by making concessions to the US, they can persuade it to abandon its hostility, they're gravely mistaken," the account added.

Last Saturday, Khamenei had called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

The Iran war has been a central issue for voters in the midterms, after U.S. President Donald Trump launched joint strikes with Israel against the Islamic Republic in February. The conflict has pushed up energy prices across the U.S., which has led to cost of living concerns.

Trump is also blaming the Iran war in part for the Democratic sweep of both chambers of Congress, telling the Wall Street Journal that "Iran was a piece of it" when asked if the war contributed to the Republicans losses.

However, he maintains that prosecuting the war was the right decision, saying, "I don't think about it. I did the right thing. I would have done it again."

Washington has not explicitly stated it plans to resume attacks on Iran, but reports from The Wall Street Journal and The New York Times indicate the U.S. is preparing for possible renewed military actions after the midterm elections.

With their new majorities, Democrats might be able to push through a War Powers Resolution, which will direct Trump to end the conflict with Iran should Congress not authorize it.

In June, both chambers — which were then Republican controlled — passed a concurrent resolution to end the conflict, although a concurrent resolution is not binding and does not have the force of law. Previous attempts to impose a binding War Powers Resolution had been defeated.

#111. UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports

The UK and EU have agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports, ahead of the UK-EU summit later this month.

The BBC understands the 200-page draft text for this so called "SPS" [sanitary and phytosanitary] agreement has been circulated to EU nations in Brussels for consideration at a meeting on Wednesday.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated alongside a 15-page draft joint statement for UK and EU top leaders.

The food deal is the most significant part of the so-called Brexit reset agreed so far, removing checks and red tape that hit exporters after the UK exited the EU's single market.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

Speaking during the Commonwealth Heads of Government Meeting (Chogm) in Antigua and Barbuda, the prime minister said a UK-EU deal could add up to 0.4% extra on GDP.

The deal could have the following impact:

Exports to the EU of fresh sausages, burgers, shellfish from domestic waters and seed potatoes would be able to resume

It should remove the need for Â£200 Export Health Certificates, which confirm the product meets the health requirements of the country it is heading to

It should also end the need for Â£25 Phytosanitary certificates, which show that plants or plant products are free from pests and diseases

Inspector fees, organic certificates, some port fees and other checks for exports and imports to and from the EU could be removed

It will bring back passports for British pets being taken into the EU

In return, the UK is expected to commit to observing and adapting to existing EU regulations on plant, animal and food trade standards - a process called "dynamic alignment".

Each of the elements on the table was up for discussion under Sir Keir Starmer, and the summit would have taken place by now had Labour not replaced its leader in July.

Under Andy Burnham, Britain's approach to post-Brexit relations with the EU have shifted significantly. Sir Keir said the UK would not rejoin the bloc in his lifetime, while Burnham has said he would like to see Britain back in the EU in his.

At Chogm on Tuesday, Burnham said: "I think people in Britain would expect me to take that with both hands, given where we've been in recent times, and that's what I'm doing."

Pressed on whether it would be acceptable for the UK to have its rules on food trade made by the EU, he said: "We're not in the EU, therefore we do have to work with them in a different way.

"But if that can get growth, if that can help our farmers, if that can remove red tape from British businesses, I think most people would expect me to go to that summit and negotiate the best deal that I can.

"And that's what I've been doing in recent weeks, leaning in, being positive, having a really constructive relationship with our European partners and hopefully we can land a good deal for Britain in terms of growth but also I hope more collaboration on controlling immigration."

Jon Walsh of Bio&Me said his firm had stopped exporting yoghurt due to vets' fees

Jon Walsh, the chief executive and co-founder of Bio&Me, which makes granolas, mueslis and yoghurts, told BBC 5 Live the draft deal sounded like "really good news".

"We're just a small company but when we were trying to export our yoghurts before, every time we'd send a pallet we had to pay a vet to inspect it and that was Â£140, and that meant that you'd lose money... so we had to stop."

The company also had problems exporting granola and overnight oats, with some shipments being returned.

"It was so frustrating because it was the same person filling out the same form and yet half would make it through and half wouldn't," Walsh said.

"You just kind of give up because you're losing money."

In an interview with ITV, Burnham said "public opinion has changed nationally" since the 2016 Brexit vote, but added he would "not run ahead of public opinion" on the issue.

"The public need to be the boss when it comes to whatever happens with Brexit," he added.

Burnham also said he had not proposed the return of freedom of movement between the UK and EU.

Asked by the BBC about the UK's relationship with the EU, Tory Leader Kemi Badenoch said that while we should "work closely", "dynamic alignment" with the EU would be a red line and "actually take us backwards".

Liberal Democrat Europe spokesperson Al Pinkerton said the draft deal was a "patch, not a fix".

"The real solution is to rejoin the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU," he added.

Reform UK's business spokesman Richard Tice said: "One of the key benefits of Brexit was that Britain no longer had to adhere to ridiculous EU rules.

"Returning to any form of alignment with Europe would crush Britain's competitiveness and force us to surrender sovereignty over trade policy, putting trade deals worth hundreds of billions of pounds at risk."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen with Prime Minister Andy Burnham

While UK rules remain mostly aligned with EU ones, there are some areas where England has diverged from the EU rule book, for example on "precision breeding" of plants, previously known as gene editing. In recent weeks, agreement has been struck on transition arrangements while the EU's new regulations come in to force.

The government has already announced a European Partnerships Bill which will provide a legal mechanism for the application of new EU rules and regulations. This will also allow for the necessary alignments for the linking of the UK emissions trading scheme with the EU's, and save the need for UK exporters to pay the EU's new carbon border tax.

The draft agreements will be discussed at a meeting of EU ambassadors called Coreper on Wednesday, with some key details on the planned numbers in a Youth Experience Scheme to be finalised at a political level in the coming days. A discount on full foreign student fees for EU students is likely to be pushed to next year alongside a plan to reconnect electricity markets.

The BBC understands the draft agreements will also include some references to UK involvement in defence co-operation schemes, access to law enforcement databases and some reassurances over the EU's "Made in Europe" plans.

A food deal will have particular significance for Northern Ireland, radically reducing the impact of the post-Brexit Irish Sea border.

Northern Ireland's Brexit deal kept it inside the EU's single market for goods. That means food products from elsewhere in the UK require paperwork and some physical checks when arriving at NI's ports. A deal will ultimately end those requirements.

While the food trade plans have been backed by supermarkets and farmers representatives, some pro-Brexit campaigners have criticised the reapplication of EU rules. The post-Brexit trade deal signed by Boris Johnson's government prioritised regulatory freedoms for the UK over avoiding new regulatory red tape, checks and delays.

But William Bain, the British Chamber of Commerce's (BCC) head of trade policy, told the BBC's Today programme that business overall thought it was a "price is worth paying" to follow common rules, as companies would benefit from "lower costs, reduced delays [and] increased market access".

At the moment, he said, the requirement for Export Health Certificates can add thousands of pounds of costs to each truck carrying goods, in addition "to the delays that people find at the ports or at the borders".

"So we get lower exports because of the delays and the massive increases in costs that have come into the supply chain."

The BCC has released a new analysis of its members' views suggesting options for a closer relationship.

The business lobby group says the current trade relationship is "failing to deliver", leaving firms "struggling with a thicket of Brexit red tape" nearly six years after the UK's exit from the bloc.

#109. JetBlue raises bag fees for third time in a year as fuel costs bite

JetBlue Airways has raised the price of checking a bag for many customers for the third time in less than a year as high fuel costs continue to eat into the industry's profits.

Checking a first bag for travelers booked in economy will now be $50, up from $45, if purchased more than 24 hours before departure during a nonpeak travel period. If it's paid within 24 hours of departure, the fee will be $60. During a peak period, such as during the holidays or the summer, it will be $55 if purchased more than 24 hours before departure, up from $49, and $70 for within 24 hours of departure.

There are exceptions to the checked bag fees, such as for customers who hold a co-branded JetBlue credit card or who hold elite frequent flyer status.

"We're adjusting checked bag fees to help offset rising fuel and operating costs," JetBlue said in a statement. "This helps us keep our base fares competitive while giving customers the choice to pay for a checked bag only if they need one."

The price of jet fuel, airlines' biggest cost after labor, has roughly doubled this year since the start of the Iran war in February, costing airlines billions and driving up the price of tickets. Jet fuel was going for $4.44 a gallon on Tuesday for the U.S. Gulf of Mexico, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

Other airlines often follow when one carrier raises fees. Major U.S. airlines did so earlier this year after JetBlue raised its bag fees.

The changes were reported earlier by the Thrifty Traveler blog.

#108. Poundland owner in crunch talks with as retailer's collapse looms

Business Secretary Jonathan Reynolds will meet the US-based owners of Poundland on Wednesday as the government hopes to save the retailer from collapse.

The discount chain is on the brink of insolvency and Gordon Brothers, which bought it for Â£1 last June, has rejected a takeover bid which could have saved the 12,000 jobs currently at risk.

A meeting was due to be held on Thursday but has been brought forward as the scramble to secure a rescue deal for the firm intensifies.

Gordon Brothers has previously said Poundland faces multiple economic challenges from rising inflation, increasing staff costs, and "severe levels of shoplifting".

Both Reynolds and Prime Minister Andy Burnham have questioned why a deal for the future of the business has not been made.

Meanwhile, MPs say they have called in Poundland's owners for questioning to explain their role in the discount retailer's looming insolvency.

Liam Byrne, chair of Parliament's business committee, said: "Every report suggests Poundland is a viable, profitable business with a future ahead of it and without good reason to let it crash and burn - and then to break it up and sell it off piece by piece.

"We are asking Gordon Brothers to appear before Parliament to explain themselves and tell us just what it is that's preventing them from accepting the fully-funded deal on the table to save more than 12,000 jobs."

Business Minister Kate Dearden and union representatives are also meeting Poundland's managing director Barry Williams later today.

Poundland has filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which protects it from its lenders until next Thursday.

Adam Turner: We would lose "a few million" if Poundland went under

The meeting comes after a major Poundland supplier has said it will lose millions of pounds if the retailer goes bust.

Adam Turner, sales director at Bradford-based Highcrest Retail Brands, said Poundland was one of its biggest customers and its collapse would cost it "a few million" in lost sales.

The family owned business mainly sells confectionery to Poundland and turns over about Â£50m.

Turner said the firm becoming insolvent would leave a substantial gap in its balance sheet next year.

Highcrest was a "longstanding business that can stomach this", Turner said. But he warned other medium and small suppliers to the retailer have told him that "if Poundland goes under, so do they".

This echoes a warning from Poundland founder Steve Smith on Monday that two or three thousand suppliers to the firm, which only work for Poundland, would be directly affected by its collapse.

Not only would Poundland's 12,000 staff be affected in that case, but potentially up to 30,000 overall, he told the BBC on Wednesday.

He said he was "amazed" Poundland was being put into administration, pointing to the profitability of the company.

"Also, there's two offers on the table from some really good people," he added.

"All I'm asking is for Gordon Brothers to see if they can put a deal together."

Gordon Brothers said Walsall-based Poundland is "still not cash generative at a sustainable level" because of the economic issues it faces.

If Poundland's stores are sold to new buyers, some of the employees may get new jobs on the same premises.

However, it is understood that buyers are only interested in about half the stores at present â meaning roughly 6,000 employees would not be re-employed.

Poundland's founder offers to invest in firm

If Poundland goes under, suppliers will be getting "pennies in the pound" back from the administration, he said, which is "very upsetting" â although he still hopes a deal can be done so Poundland can remain solvent.

Poundland owners Gordon Brothers will be a secured creditor, which means they are first in line to get their money back, and suppliers "who have done nothing wrong" will take the financial hit.

"It's sad. It's a nightmare for us all to be honest with you. It's not just the suppliers, it's the staff at Poundland, some of these guys have been there for many years.

"It's from the warehouse staff all the way to the shop staff to everybody within head office. It's a massive ripple effect through the supply chain," he added.

Turner said he had no knowledge of whether Gordon Brothers had deliberately timed the process of Poundland going into administration so it could take advantage of having high stock levels, which it can then sell.

One of the ways Gordon Brothers makes money is through stock liquidations â selling off stock from collapsed businesses.

One insider familiar with the situation told the BBC that the timing of Poundland facing collapse was "odd" â most retailers don't fall over in the run-up to Christmas.

Dearden has said the government doesn't want to interfere with "current ongoing commercial negotiations", but that ministers want "clarity" on the plans for the retailer.

"It's absolutely legitimate for this government to ask the business if they're doing everything they can to protect employees and suppliers up and down the country," she added.

Dearden told MPs that the government would be ready to help the company's employees if Poundland collapses.

The head of Gordon Brothers' European operations reportedly told the government that the firm stood by its stewardship and had given Poundland Â£85m in additional financing since its purchase in 2025.

#106. AI data center stocks and Musk's SpaceX decline as Texas governor result raises risks of a slowdown

AI data center stocks came under pressure as Democrats — seen as proponents of moratoriums on the buildout — won big in midterm elections and in key technology hubs like Texas.

Markets were already anticipating the Democrats to win the House of Representatives and possibly take Senate control. But the Texas governor mansion going to Democrats was a big blow, analysts said. Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who wants to enact an immediate moratorium on data centers in the state, won over incumbent Republican Gov. Greg Abbott. According to Wells Fargo, 27% of planned data center gigawatts is in the Lone Star State.

The midterm elections will slow the pace of data center buildouts across the U.S., Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"There is no way to sugarcoat that Democrats taking control especially over the Senate will create some major hurdles for AI and tech legislation in the Beltway over the next 2 years," he said.

He added that the election in Texas was the "biggest worry" of the night for tech investors and the AI trade because that's where around 25%-30% of data center gigawatts and capacity will come from.

Shares of Elon Musk's SpaceX shed 7%. The Democrats' victory in Texas is a big setback for Musk, where he heavily backed Republican candidates, and where his companies Tesla and SpaceX are based. Musk spent a record of at least $474 million on the midterms and funneled $224 million into Texas alone, where Republican Senate nominee Ken Paxton and Gov. Greg Abbott lost to Democrats.

SpaceX is currently building out multiple gigawatts of data center capacity, including projects in Texas.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk said in a post on X in the early hours on Wednesday, referring to his speculative plans to build AI infrastructure off-planet.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Iren fell by 7% and 6%, respectively. Peer Nebius Group was down 3% and Oracle shed 4.3%. Nvidia shares were slightly in the red.

Tech giants Alphabet, Meta and Microsoft mostly bucked the trend and were higher.

The Democratic win in Texas adds to existing pressure on the bull-market-leading AI trade. The Nasdaq-100 index fell into correction territory to end October as concerns mounted that the growth of data center spending by big hyperscalers and private AI juggernauts OpenAI and Anthropic was slowing.

"We believe the equity market reaction from the midterms mostly depends on the outcome of the Texas governor's race — a Dem win could potentially be a left-tail event to the AI trade, warned Ohsung Kwon, Wells Fargo's chief equity strategist, in a report this week.

Democrats won or retained the governorships in other key data center states Georgia, Pennsylvania and Ohio. Georgia represents 7% of all the planned U.S. datacenter gigawatts, while Ohio and Pennsylvania account for 5% apiece, estimates Wells Fargo.

Notable investment strategist Michael Hartnett of Bank of America warned earlier this year that Democrats winning the Senate, combined with taking the Texas governorship could lead to a "big slump" in stocks of more than 10% into year-end.

"Data centers went from being a popular economic development tool in 2024 and 2025 to something of a pariah by the time the midterm campaigns gathered steam," wrote the Washington policy team at Optimal Advisory this week. "The midterms are likely to send a message to local and state officials that there is a political upside to blocking, delaying and/or raising the cost of data centers."

#105. Wayfair sales jump 10% as furniture retailer says it's winning share of sluggish market

Wayfair on Wednesday said it posted its strongest quarter of sales growth since the pandemic as the furniture retailer continues to win over customers and take market share.

The company said it saw double-digit revenue growth in the U.S., reaching about $3 billion, an 11.4% increase from the year prior. Still, international net revenue for the quarter decreased 2.3% year over year to $380 million.

The report comes as the housing market remains sluggish. But the furniture and home goods retailer has reported consistent growth over the past year. CFO Kate Gulliver told CNBC that Wayfair's momentum is building as it takes market share.

"We see the overall consumer in the U.S. in this category, we think it's roughly flattish, and so that's our [market] share momentum continuing to build," Gulliver said.

She added that shopper loyalty, the opening of new stores, the company's luxury furniture business and an emphasis on value has allowed the company to win more consumers. Customers are also continuing to spend, whether at the company's value prices or on the luxury side, she added.

"We've been highly promotional for the last few years, and the promotional activity this year is actually very consistent with last year," she said. "It maintains this sort of momentum on promotional activity this year."

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the period ended Sept. 30, Wayfair reported net income of $62 million, or 45 cents per share, compared with a loss of $99 million, or a loss of 76 cents per share, the year prior.

Total revenue grew 9.7% year over year, reaching $3.42 billion.

The company said its active customers grew 4.7% year over year, reaching 22.2 million. It also saw a 12.2% increase in orders delivered during the quarter to about 11 million.

As the company looks ahead to the holiday season and the fourth quarter, Gulliver said it's maintaining promotional activity similar to last year. She added that the company is confident that its range of prices and products will appeal to a broad base of consumers.

"We've seen that growth play out," Gulliver said. "We've seen that flow through to EBITDA, ultimately to net income positive this quarter, which is really another key piece in the story and the momentum here, so we feel very good about the quarter of the performance."

#102. McDonald's earnings beat, but traffic to its U.S. restaurants falls

McDonald's on Wednesday reported mixed quarterly results as traffic to its U.S. restaurants fell, leading to a slight decline in domestic same-store sales.

The fast-food giant reported a 0.2% decrease in sales at its U.S. restaurants that have been open at least 13 months. Even so, the segment performed better than the 0.5% decline that Wall Street anticipated, according to a survey of analysts by StreetAccount.

At the company's investor day in September, executives said that same-store sales in July and August were slightly negative but they expected September to return to same-store sales growth.

"In the U.S., we're encouraged by improvement as the quarter progressed following our intervention on value," McDonald's CEO Chris Kempczinski said in a statement.

Kempczinski said in August that the company's U.S. business was falling short on execution, particularly on the implementation of its value offerings. Like many restaurants, McDonald's has leaned into discounts to attract budget-conscious diners over the last two years.

Shares of the company rose more than 2% in premarket trading.

Here's what the company reported compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

The company reported third-quarter net income of $2.49 billion, or $3.51 per share, up from $2.28 billion, or $3.18 per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring charges and other items, McDonald's earned $3.57 per share.

The company's same-store sales rose 1.5%, lifted by its international segments. That topped analysts' expectations of a 1.3% increase, according to StreetAccount.

McDonald's international developmental licensed markets business, which includes Japan and China, reported same-store sales growth of 4.2%. Wall Street was forecasting same-store sales to rise 2.1% for the division, based on StreetAccount estimates.

The company's international operated division, which includes top markets like Australia and Germany, saw same-store sales growth of 1.4%. Still, the business underperformed Wall Street's expectations. Analysts were projecting same-store sales would rise 2.1%, according to StreetAccount. France was a weak spot, McDonald's said.

#97. Private companies added 86,000 jobs in October, slightly higher than expected, ADP says

Private sector job growth held steady in October, with gains spread across sectors, as the labor market showed fresh signs of stabilization, ADP reported Wednesday.

The payrolls processing firm said companies added 86,000 jobs during the month, the same as the downwardly revised total for September and slightly ahead of the Dow Jones estimate for 75,000.

Employment gains came mainly in the service sector.

Trade, transportation and utilities led with 39,000 new jobs, while education and health services added 28,000, and leisure and hospitality contributed 19,000. Other sectors showing gains included financial activities (15,000), construction (13,000) and other services (10,000).

However, some of the gains were offset by a decline of 27,000 in professional and business services and a loss of 8,000 in natural resources and mining. Information services, a sector potentially impacted by the artificial intelligence buildout, lost 2,000 jobs.

Salaries continued to show gains, particularly for those changing jobs — those workers saw a 4.8% annual gain. A report Tuesday from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed that the number of people quitting their job, a sign of labor market mobility, hit its highest level since March 2025.

Workers overall saw a 3.2% base pay gain, while gross pay increased 4.7% annually. Pay for those staying in their jobs rose 3%.

"Employers of all sizes were hiring last month, and our data suggests that job-changers were lured by a combination of bonuses, overtime, and more hours in addition to higher base pay," said ADP's chief economist, Nela Richardson. "Taken together, earnings growth for these workers hit its fastest pace since March 2024."

Companies with between 50 and 499 workers added 33,000 jobs. Large businesses added 24,000, while small firms increased rolls by 28,000.

The ADP report serves as a precursor to the BLS nonfarm payrolls count to be released Friday.

Economists surveyed by Dow Jones are looking for the BLS report for October to show a gain of 59,000 after September's modest growth of 29,000.

#93. Uber beats on revenue even as mobility and delivery units miss estimates

Uber reported revenue for the third quarter that topped analysts' estimates, but the company missed expectations in its two key businesses.

Here's how the company did versus analysts' estimates compiled by LSEG:

Revenue increased 10% from $13.47 billion a year earlier. The company reported net income of $512 million, down 92% from a year earlier, when the company had a $4.9 billion benefit from a tax valuation release.

Uber's core mobility unit, which is its ride-hailing service, saw a 2% decline in revenue to $7.52 billion, missing the average estimate of $7.71 billion, according to StreetAccount. Revenue in the food delivery business increased 23% to $5.49 billion, trailing the $5.51 billion average estimate.

Total bookings of $59.82 billion were about in line with the $59.83 billion average analyst estimate, according to StreetAccount.

Uber's revenue beat was due to growth in its freight division. Sales jumped 34% to $1.76 billion, topping the StreetAccount consensus of $1.49 billion,

Uber CEO Dara Khosrowshahi said, in prepared remarks ahead of a scheduled call, that the company's core mobility segment performed slightly below expectations overseas.

"Elevated fuel prices and tighter driver availability weighed on supply growth in several international markets," he said, adding that Uber is making "targeted investments to strengthen driver supply."

The company plans to have over 2,000 automated vehicles available on its platform by the end of 2027, half operating in the U.S. and half internationally. It expects to have around 700 available on its platform by the end of the year, adding Amazon's Zoox and Motional robotaxis to its platform in Las Vegas this quarter.

Uber is also growing its delivery business through acquisitions of Delivery Hero in Germany and ezCater in the U.S.

In its Uber Eats business, a recently expanded partnership with Costco in the U.S. is "tracking ahead of expectations," Khosrowshahi said. The company said Costco is now one of its top merchants in terms of gross bookings.

For the fourth quarter, Uber sees overall bookings of $64.5 billion at the mid-point of its forecast, in line with the average StreetAccount estimate of $64.26 billion.

#89. CVS to split off drugstore business in major breakup of healthcare giant

CVS Health plans to split into two separate public companies by the first half 2028 in what would be one of the largest corporate splits in history, the company announced Wednesday.

The move would reverse the company's massive $78 billion acquisition of its insurer, Aetna, and dismantle the vertically integrated healthcare conglomerate it created in 2018. The plan would specifically separate CVS's roughly 9,000 retail pharmacies from its major insurance business and pharmacy benefit manager, Caremark, one of the nation's three largest PBMs.

The decision follows a "comprehensive strategic review" by the company's board of directors, according to a news release.

It's a stunning reversal for the healthcare powerhouse, which has spent the last two decades working to turn itself into a one-stop health destination. The move also comes as CVS's aggressive turnaround plan under CEO David Joyner shows substantial progress, with the company pursuing $2 billion in cost cuts, store closures, leadership shakeups and efforts to rein in Medicare Advantage costs.

"From a position of strength, and a focus on the long term, we plan to create two focused companies to improve consumer engagement, affordability, and care coordination," Joyner said in the release.

Shares have jumped roughly 70% since Joyner took the helm in October 2024, bringing the company's market value to $117 billion.

The two new companies would both be publicly traded.

The insurance and PBM company will retain the CVS Health name and be run by Joyner, while the other company will be called CVS Pharmacy and be led by Prem Shah, who is currently group president of CVS Health. Additional leadership positions and board members will be announced at a later date, CVS said.

Existing CVS shareholders will own shares in both companies after the split. The Financial Times on Tuesday first reported the breakup plans.

The company's pharmacy business raked in $37.66 billion in 2025 sales, while its insurance unit booked $36.29 billion in revenue and healthcare services segment generated $51.24 billion.

CVS on Wednesday also posted third-quarter earnings and revenue that topped Wall Street expectations. Total sales climbed to $108.40 billion, from $102.87 billion in the prior-year period.

Sales were boosted by year-over-year growth in the company's health care benefits and health services segments. Meanwhile, revenue in its pharmacy services business slid from the previous year.

On Wednesday, CVS said it will lump its insurer and PBM with its primary care business Oak Street Health, home-visit provider Signify Health and the company's specialty drug business.

CVS also said the split will allow the two companies to experience a "focused strategic agenda" and greater ability to "innovate, adopt technology, increase efficiency," among other benefits.

It's not the first time CVS has weighed splitting the company. In October 2024, CVS engaged advisors in a strategic review of its business and considered breaking up its retail pharmacy and insurance units.

Even so, Leerink Partners analyst Michael Cherny said the split "feels like a complete shock" in a Tuesday note. But he said that the retail pharmacy business has been performing well, winning market share from competitors and seeing stabilizing gross margins thanks to CostVantage, a new pharmacy reimbursement model introduced by CVS last year.

"In this context, perhaps the outlook for a standalone retail pharmacy (still with meaningful scale and leverage on its own) is stronger now than before," Cherny wrote.

He added that "the rationale could also be to avoid what has been relentless state legislative scrutiny over the company's level of vertical integration."

The potential breakup also comes as pharmacy benefit managers face mounting bipartisan scrutiny in Washington, D.C., and across states.

PBMs, which negotiate drug prices and manage prescription drug benefits on behalf of insurers and employers, have been criticized for opaque pricing practices, their growing market power and potential conflicts of interest when they are owned by companies that also operate insurers and pharmacies.

Congress enacted PBM reforms in February, including provisions to change how PBMs are compensated under Medicare Part D. Meanwhile, states have increasingly moved to restrict PBMs' ownership of pharmacies.

#85. SpaceX signs deal with Qualcomm to provide network technology for U.S. mobile service

SpaceX announced a deal with chipmaker Qualcomm to provide key network technology for Elon Musk's Starlink Mobile service, a challenger to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile.

Qualcomm said on Wednesday that the terrestrial network it will help SpaceX build will "complement Starlink's satellite capabilities," with a planned rollout to begin next year. Financial terms of the multiyear deal weren't disclosed.

"Together with SpaceX, we are building world-leading cellular infrastructure in the United States, advancing 5G today, with an opportunity to lay the foundation for AI-native 6G in the years ahead," Durga Malladi, an executive vice president at Qualcomm, said in the statement.

Starlink is a critical piece of SpaceX's business, accounting for 36% of the company's revenue in the third quarter and 85% of its operating profit. But to date, the company's consumer business has been limited when it comes to mobile direct-to-device services as its satellite network can't provide all the voice, video and data coverage that competitors offer on popular smartphones.

Now SpaceX is snapping up spectrum and forging partnerships to take on the major mobile carriers. Last month, the company announced an agreement to purchase a nationwide spectrum portfolio in the 800 MHz band. The news sent shares of U.S. carriers reeling. Earlier this week, the Washington Post reported that SpaceX is in talks to acquire EchoStar's Boost Mobile.

SpaceX went public in June in the largest IPO on record and is now valued at close to $2 trillion.

With Qualcomm, SpaceX will be using the chipmaker's Dragonwing cellular infrastructure hardware and software in base stations to enable high-capacity coverage for Starlink Mobile.

Qualcomm has a long history of making chips for cellular networks, but is best known for making the modems and radios that go inside devices, not base stations for running a wireless network. The company has targeted carriers and other infrastructure companies since it made a bigger push into the 5G infrastructure market in 2020.

SpaceX represents one of Qualcomm's highest-profile customers or partners in 5G infrastructure, following earlier deals with Verizon, Dish, and Europe's Vodafone.

While SpaceX plans to use Qualcomm's technology to establish tens of thousands of new cell sites, it hasn't announced partners among cell tower companies or said how many new towers may be required.

Mobile carriers generally lease space from companies such as American Tower and Crown Castle, which commonly host multiple carriers from one site.

SpaceX did not respond to CNBC's request for additional information.

-- CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed reporting.
